# Contractor Coordination Platform — Project Source of Truth

> **Living document.** This is the single source of truth for the project: what we're
> building, how it's structured, what's decided, what's still open, what's done, and a
> running log of every bug and every decision *with the reason behind it*.
> Update it **as we go** — before or right after a change, never "later."
>
> Codename: _TBD_ · Owner: _(you)_ · Stack: React (Vite) · Node/Express · MongoDB/Mongoose · JWT · Multer
> Last updated: **2026-08-29**
>
> **Companion document:** the full database design lives in [`docs/database-design.html`](docs/database-design.html)
> — an interactive entity map, per-collection schemas, and the reasoning behind every modelling choice.
> This MD stays the source of truth for *decisions*; that file is the source of truth for the *schema*.
>
> **Where this document lives:** the MD is maintained on **`develop`** and **`main`** only, and is kept
> byte-identical on both — an update is committed on `develop`, then `main` is fast-forwarded to that same
> commit. `feature/*` branches intentionally trail behind by documentation-only commits; that is **expected
> and correct, not drift**. A feature branch picks up the current MD when it merges into `develop`.

## How to use this document

**Status markers** (used in the to-do list and feature registry):

| Marker | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `Planned` | Agreed, not started |
| `In progress` | Actively being built |
| `Done` | Built **and** tested |
| `Blocked` | Waiting on a decision or a fix |
| `Cut` | Deliberately dropped (always record why) |

Use `[ ]` / `[x]` checkboxes inside task lists.

**Three golden rules:**

1. **Every change** to the project (code, structure, or scope) gets a one-line entry in the
   [Decision Log](#11-decision-log) with the *reason*. Future-you needs to know *why*, not just *what*.
2. **Every bug or problem solved** gets an entry in the [Bug & Issue Log](#10-bug--issue-log),
   so we never debug the same thing twice.
3. **Every change that touches the data model updates
   [`docs/database-design.html`](docs/database-design.html) in the same sitting** — a new collection,
   a new or renamed field, a changed relationship, an added index, a resolved open decision, or a new
   privacy/visibility rule. That file is a teaching document *and* the schema reference; a stale one is
   worse than none, because it will be trusted. It is data-driven: the whole page renders from the
   `COLLECTIONS` array (plus `FLOWS`, `TASK_FIELDS`, `OPEN_DECISIONS`…) near the top of its single
   `<script>` block, so an update usually means editing one object, not touching any markup.

**Definition of "Done":** it works, it's tested, and it didn't break existing behavior or drift
the design. Two extra gates:

- Anything touching **visibility/privacy** gets an explicit *"who must NOT see this?"* check.
- Anything with a **UI** is checked in **both languages and both directions** — Hebrew RTL *and*
  English LTR — before it's marked Done. A screen that only works in one direction is not Done. See [§3.4](#34-bilingual--bidirectional-ui-hebrew-rtl-default--english-ltr).

---

## 1. Product overview

**The problem.** A general contractor (GC) running a construction project spends the day chasing
subcontractors and suppliers. When one slips — the drilling crew is two days late — every dependent
task downstream has to be renegotiated by phone, one call at a time. The GC ends up coordinating
instead of running the project.

**The solution.** A coordination platform where tasks have explicit dependencies. When a task moves,
the system cascades the change to affected tasks, proposes new dates to everyone impacted, and lets
each party **approve / reject / counter-propose** — in a bounded negotiation — inside the system
instead of over the phone. Everything is logged.

**What makes it more than "Trello for construction":**

- **Equal users.** Everyone is a first-class user — GCs, subs, suppliers. Roles are *not* fixed to a
  person: the same contractor can be GC on one project and a sub on another. The platform mediates
  relationships; it doesn't serve one side.
- **Confidential delegation.** (Field-confirmed as critical.) When a sub can't make a date, they
  often quietly send another contractor in their place — and the GC isn't supposed to know who's
  actually on site. The platform lets a contractor delegate work to a collaborator **invisibly to the
  party above them**. The GC sees only the sub they contracted with. The same model lets a contractor
  privately manage their own execution (sub-tasks, notes, team) hidden from the GC.
- **Trust signals that aren't gameable.** The **flexibility score** is auto-calculated from how a
  contractor actually behaves on reschedules (accept / reject / counter) — not self-reported.
- **Request-gated messaging.** Anyone may write first. A first message from someone the recipient is
  not connected to arrives as a **request**, and the recipient **approves** it — which opens the
  thread — **rejects** it, or **blocks** the sender, which is the no-contact state. **Connections are
  the network layer, not the messaging gate.** The anti-spam argument is unchanged but now sits
  somewhere else: spam control is **recipient-owned** through the request gate, so nothing becomes a
  conversation in an inbox until the person on the other end allows it — which is what stops the
  platform becoming a supplier sales-spam channel. ~~LinkedIn-style: request a connection → they
  accept → then you can message.~~ *(Superseded — the connection gate was **reversed** by the
  validated findings of 2026-08-28; see [D9](#7-open-decisions) and [§11](#11-decision-log).)*
- **Hebrew-first and genuinely bilingual.** The people using this are on Israeli job sites, so Hebrew
  (**RTL**) is the default language *and* the default reading direction, with English (**LTR**) as a
  complete second language rather than a fallback. This is a product requirement, not polish: a
  subcontractor who can't comfortably read the screen won't use the platform, and the whole value
  depends on *every* party being in the system. Full rules in [§3.4](#34-bilingual--bidirectional-ui-hebrew-rtl-default--english-ltr).

**Deliberate non-goals** (these protect the trust model — don't "helpfully" add them later):

- **No pricing or money flow.** Rates are confidential in this industry; touching them kills adoption.
- **No contract templates.** Every job is different — the platform just exchanges files.
- **No progress nagging.** Binary **start / complete** only. Contractors are on job sites, not at desks.
  *One reconciliation, so the next reader does not "fix" one validated rule against another:* the
  replacement flow **does** record how much of the work was already done — `כן צריך לתעד כמה מהעבודה
  כבר בוצעה` — but that is a **one-time, event-scoped handover record**, written when a professional
  is replaced mid-work so the successor knows what he is inheriting. It is **not progress tracking**,
  it is not asked of anyone routinely, and **the binary day-to-day model is untouched**.
- **The system never manages the contractor.** Validated 2026-08-28 and recorded verbatim:
  `אסור להגיע למצב שבו המערכת מנהלת את הקבלן!` It is a top-level design principle, and it is a
  non-goal like the three above. **Four decisions the platform must never take automatically on a
  contractor's behalf:** `תחילת עבודה` (starting work) · `דחיית עבודה` (delaying work) ·
  `בחירת ספקים` (choosing suppliers) · `בחירת קבלני משנה` (choosing subcontractors). **And the
  companion rule from the same delivery:** `הקבלן הראשי חייב לראות תגובות של קבלנים` — the GC must
  see contractors' responses, specifically **work declined, work started, and a change of
  work-course**. This already governs rules closed elsewhere in this document — the 72-hour proposal
  expiry does nothing automatic and hands the decision back to the GC ([D2](#7-open-decisions)),
  upstream-finished routes to the GC rather than releasing the dependent, and automatic statuses are
  *records* rather than decisions — so nothing built today violates it. What it binds is what comes
  next, the rules engine below.

**Stretch goal (only if time allows):** a personal rules engine — contractors configure automatic
actions on top of the coordination layer. **Its automatic actions may never include the four
never-automate decisions above**, and its notifications must preserve the GC-must-see set — that
constraint binds the feature before it is designed, not after.

---

## 2. Tech stack

| Layer | Tech |
|---|---|
| Frontend | React (Vite), React Router, Axios, **Context API** + **Redux**, MUI |
| Backend | Node.js, Express, `express-rate-limit` |
| Database | MongoDB + Mongoose |
| Auth | JWT, bcrypt |
| Email | **Nodemailer over Brevo SMTP** — chosen 2026-08-29. Brevo is the service, Nodemailer the client; ordinary authenticated SMTP, so no vendor SDK and no API key in the application. Credentials come from the environment, all-or-nothing, and an unconfigured server runs in an explicit log mode rather than appearing to send |
| File upload | **Multer → GridFS** — multipart parsing, type and size validation, then streamed into GridFS, with `fileassets` as the metadata and ownership layer ([D1](#7-open-decisions), closed 2026-08-29). Built 2026-08-29 |
| Location & routing | **Google Places** (Place IDs) for structured location selection and discovery, and **Google Routes — Compute Route Matrix** for distance. Chosen 2026-08-29 by owner decision, and the reason is the product rule rather than the API: a travel radius is **real driving distance, never straight-line**, so it cannot be computed in the application. **Quota, caching and the query design are not decided** |
| Localization | Hebrew (**RTL**, default) + English (**LTR**) — mechanism under React is [D10](#7-open-decisions) |
| Deployment | Client → Vercel (or Netlify); Server → Heroku |

**On MUI**, added 2026-08-29 by owner instruction and deliberately kept to one thing: the
in-button `CircularProgress` on the auth screens. No `ThemeProvider`, no `CssBaseline`, no MUI form
controls and no MUI layout — the spinner takes `color="inherit"` so it renders in the FieldSync
palette rather than importing a second design system into one that already exists. The rule worth
holding is that MUI is a component source here, not the styling layer.

---

## 3. Architecture & main flow

> This section is intentionally high-level for now. It **will change** as we build. We firm up the
> data model and API surface stage by stage rather than designing everything up front.

**Big picture:**

```
React SPA  ──HTTP (Axios)──►  Express REST API  ──Mongoose──►  MongoDB
(Vercel)                       (Heroku)                         (Atlas)
```

**Auth flow (Stage 1–2):** user logs in → server verifies (bcrypt) → returns **two** tokens with
different jobs: a short-lived **Access Token** in the response body, which the client attaches to
requests (Axios interceptor) and which the auth middleware verifies on protected routes; and a
long-lived **Refresh Token** in an HttpOnly cookie, which buys a new Access Token from
`POST /api/auth/refresh` and is accepted nowhere else. The client also runs a `PrivateRoute` wrapper.
Superseded the original single-JWT flow on 2026-08-27 — see [§11](#11-decision-log).

**Core domain loop (Stage 3+):** projects contain tasks → tasks have dependencies (a DAG) → changing a
date triggers a **cascade** to affected tasks → each affected party gets a **reschedule proposal** they
can approve / reject / counter → the resolution is written to the **audit log** and feeds the
**flexibility score**. The exact cascade and negotiation semantics are an [open decision](#7-open-decisions).

**Privacy layer (Stage 5):** delegation and the private execution layer are added *on top of* a working
simple-visibility model — not built in from day one. All task data leaves the server through a single
viewer-aware serializer so visibility is enforced in exactly one place.

### 3.1 Repo structure (skeleton — grows over time)

**One repo, two folders** — [D4](#7-open-decisions) settled in practice. The folders are `Frontend/`
and `Backend/`, **not** the `client/` + `server/` this section assumed until 2026-08-27, and the
backend is organised **by domain rather than by layer**: there is no top-level `controllers/` or
`models/` holding every feature's files. A branch named after a screen adds code to the domain
module that owns the responsibility, so two screens sharing a responsibility share one module.

```
project-root/
├── Frontend/                # React (Vite) — deploys to Vercel
│   └── screens/             # static HTML/CSS prototypes + the tracked lang/validation scripts
├── Backend/                 # Node/Express + TypeScript — deploys to Heroku
│   ├── src/
│   │   ├── server.ts        process entry: env → config → database → listen → graceful shutdown
│   │   ├── app.ts           Express factory; importable without starting a listener
│   │   ├── config/          env.ts (the ONLY reader of process.env) · cors.ts
│   │   ├── db/              mongoose connect / disconnect / status
│   │   ├── middleware/      validateRequest (JOI) · notFoundHandler · errorHandler
│   │   ├── routes/          index.ts (composition root) · health · health-auth
│   │   ├── features/        one folder per domain — users/, auth/, …
│   │   └── shared/          errors.ts (AppError) · logger.ts
│   ├── requests/            .http request collections (VS Code REST Client / Postman)
│   ├── CLAUDE.md            permanent backend rules
│   └── README.md            layout, error contract, auth model
├── docs/database-design.html
└── Contractor Coordination Platform — Pro.md   # this file
```

Detail for the backend lives in [`Backend/README.md`](Backend/README.md); it is the reference for
the layout and the API error contract, and this section stays the high-level shape.

### 3.2 Data model (entity index — full design in the companion HTML)

> **This table is the index; the design lives in [`docs/database-design.html`](docs/database-design.html).**
> That file holds every field, every index, the Mongoose schema, a sample document, and the *reason* for
> each embed-vs-reference call — plus an interactive entity map, a serializer/visibility simulator, and
> step-by-step flows. The design is still confirmed stage by stage as we build (we do not pre-optimise),
> but it is no longer "TBD": it is a worked model with its assumptions written down and its remaining
> [open decisions](#7-open-decisions) mapped to the exact fields they would change.
>
> Golden rule 3 applies: **change the model, change that file in the same sitting.**

| Collection | Purpose | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `users` | Account + identity (`registrationCategory`, specialties, location, bio, avatar), computed rating + flexibility, global `isAdmin` flag | The **only** global role is `admin`. Secrets carry `select: false`. Carries `termsAcceptances[]` — the version and timestamp of every Terms consent, appended never overwritten ([D25](#7-open-decisions)). Holds `language` (`he`/`en`) — see [§3.4](#34-bilingual--bidirectional-ui-hebrew-rtl-default--english-ltr) **2026-08-29:** gained `security.passwordChangedAt`, written by a password reset and read by the auth middleware — it is what makes a *stateless* Access Token revocable, since a JWT issued before it is refused. It is the one field of the schema page's designed `security` sub-document that is implemented; `resetTokenHash` and `resetTokenExpiresAt` deliberately are not, and `emailVerifiedAt` is not built **2026-08-29, profile backend (`5ecc46c`):** the editable profile fields are now real — `bio`, `specialties` + `specialtyOther`, `businessPhone`, `location.city` / `location.region` / **`location.travelRadiusKm`** (nested inside `location`, where the schema page had designed it beside), `schedulingPrefs.delayToleranceDays` / `noticeRequiredDays` (bounds enforced, **no defaults** — an unset preference has to read as unset), and **`avatar.fileId` only**. The schema page's denormalised `avatar.url` was **removed rather than implemented**: the URL is derived at response time from the id, so there is no second copy to go stale. ~~**Heavy equipment is still not here** and was deliberately not invented~~ — **superseded 2026-08-29 (owner-QA corrections): `heavyEquipment` is now a real field**, a top-level array of the ten approved machine codes, refining the `heavy_equipment` trade the way `specialtyOther` refines `other`. **The service clears it whenever that trade is not among the specialties the patch leaves behind**, so a machine list can never outlive the trade that carries it, and an empty array is a real answer rather than a missing one. Not indexed, not a filter, and not on the public profile — none of those has been approved. **The shape is the narrowest of the three [D14](#7-open-decisions) named and is an implementation choice awaiting ratification, not a closed decision** — see [D14](#7-open-decisions) **2026-08-29, the travel model (owner):** `location.travelRadiusKm` is **real driving distance**, never straight-line, computed with **Google Routes (Compute Route Matrix)** — so the field is a query input to an external service rather than a number the application can evaluate on its own. It is joined by a second preference, **`approvedTravelLocations`**: the places the contractor will actually travel to, selected through **Google Places** with Place IDs, where the generated places are only a **suggested list and the user's manually edited list is authoritative** — a suggestion may be removed, and a place **outside the automatic radius** may be added. ~~**Kept open exactly, and nothing here chooses any of it:** the **schema placement** of `approvedTravelLocations`, the **stored shape of a Place-ID entry**, and **quota / caching** for the two APIs~~ — **the first two were settled by implementation on 2026-08-29 (Browse backend); quota / caching remains open.** **As built:** `approvedTravelLocations` is a **top-level array on [[users]]**, not inside `location`. Each entry stores `placeId`, `displayName`, `latitude`, `longitude`, `source` (`suggested` | `manual`), `approvedAt`, and an optional `drivingDistanceMeters`. A multikey index on `approvedTravelLocations.placeId` serves the explicit-place Browse filter. **It is the authoritative list of places the contractor is willing to work in, and nothing recomputes it on save** — which is what makes a removal stick and a manually added place outside the radius stay approved **New the same day — `excludedTravelLocations`:** a person's removal is a **standing decision**, not a dismissal. Entries are `placeId`, `displayName`, `excludedAt`; the name is kept so the editor can show the decision back without a second Google call. The radius proposal reads this list and **never suggests those places again at any radius** — Google may rediscover one, and it is returned in a separate `previouslyRemoved` bucket rather than silently dropped, so the proposal is not lossy. **Approving beats excluding**: naming a place in `approvedTravelLocations` clears its exclusion, which makes an explicit manual re-add the one way back. **Private to the person** — it is in no public projection. No index: it is only ever read for its own owner by `_id` **New the same day — `location.place`:** the structured Google place behind the free-text `city`, stored as `placeId`, `displayName`, `latitude`, `longitude` and optional `city`/`adminArea`. **Optional by design.** `city` and `region` remain stored and displayed, so a legacy account that only ever typed a city stays readable — and **no Place ID is ever fabricated from a `city` string**. It is what the driving-distance filter measures from, so a contractor without one cannot be measured and is reported through `distanceFilterDegraded` rather than treated as far away |
| `companies` | The contractor business a user operates through: name, office phone, and whether the **organization** is accepting new work | **Added 2026-08-28** with the Register backend, closing [D28](#7-open-decisions)'s storage half. A User is a person, a Company is the business. `name` is **required at owner registration, public, and searchable by users** (approved 2026-08-28, closing [D28](#7-open-decisions)) — and still **not unique**. `availability` is the organization's **work** availability and lives here, **not** on each employee — it must never be reinterpreted as personal availability. No owner field: ownership is a [[companymemberships]] row **2026-08-29, employee-management backend:** gained **`employeeSetupCompletedAt`** — absent/null means this business has not yet completed *or skipped* the optional first-time Employee Management onboarding step, a timestamp means it has. It lives on the **Company** because a company's staff is what was set up: on the person it would be a fact about whoever signed up first, and a second manager joining later would be walked through the step again for a company that already has staff. A date rather than a boolean, because it also answers *when*. **Skip and Finish record the same fact** and both write it; the write is **idempotent and keeps the first stamp**, so a later call cannot rewrite the day it happened. **No `localStorage` or other client flag participates** — the browser never decides whether the step is done |
| `companymemberships` | One document per person × company relationship: `standing` (owner/employee), `status`, `companyPosition`, `permissions` | **Added 2026-08-28.** It exists because **an invitation has no user** — an owner opens a seat with a name and a position before any account exists, so the relationship cannot be fields on a person. A User is never an "owner account" or an "employee account". `{ company, status }` serves the pending-activation list ~~Partial unique index = **one active relationship per person**~~ — **superseded 2026-08-29, see `user_current_unique` below** **The lifecycle is now built, not just modelled (2026-08-29):** an owner opens a seat (`invited`, no user), an employee's self-registration is matched on *company name + `invitedFullName` + `companyPosition`* and claims it (`pending_company_approval`), and the owner approves it (`active`) one at a time or all at once. The name alone grants nothing — it only narrows the search — and a registration matching no seat writes nothing at all **2026-08-29, later the same day:** `companyPosition` gained a sixth code, `regional_construction_manager`, naming a role the terminology already distinguished in words — the vocabulary now reads Construction Manager / Regional Construction Manager / Site Manager, which is the distinction [D28](#7-open-decisions) draws. The codes are **stable identifiers and are never renamed to follow a label**: the Hebrew wording for a site manager was reworded twice while `site_manager` stayed exactly what it was — and on 2026-08-29 `main_contractor`'s Hebrew label moved from `קבלן ראשי` to **`קבלן ביצוע ראשי`** while the code did not move at all **2026-08-29, one current company (approved):** a person belongs to **exactly one company at a time**. The two states that mean *in a company* are `pending_company_approval` and `active`, and **`user_current_unique`** — unique on `{ user }`, partial over those two statuses — makes *active at A + pending at B*, two pending rows and two active rows all unstorable. An unclaimed `invited` seat carries **no user** and an `inactive` row is **history**, so both sit outside the constraint: a company may hold any number of open seats, and somebody who genuinely left may later join elsewhere. **An ended membership is not a current company** — it never becomes a company context, and its `permissions` array is **kept for history and audit and deliberately never cleared**, which is why the read is what enforces the distinction: *stored historically ≠ currently authorized* **2026-08-29, the owner holds a position (owner-approved):** a company owner created by Register is that company's **Main Contractor**, and registration now writes `companyPosition: 'main_contractor'` on the owner row. ~~`companyPosition` is left unset for an owner~~ is **superseded** — it was never true of the product, only of the code. The internal key is unchanged (`main_contractor`); the labels stay `קבלן ביצוע ראשי` / `Main Contractor`. A **narrow, idempotent backfill** (`npm run migrate:owner-main-contractor`, `--dry-run` supported) exists for rows created before this: it touches **only** `standing: 'owner'` rows with **no position at all**, and never rewrites a row that already names a different job **Consequence — one Main Contractor per company:** opening a second seat for that position is refused **409 `MAIN_CONTRACTOR_SEAT_TAKEN`**. "Occupied" means any membership in `invited | pending_company_approval | active` that either names `main_contractor` **or** has `standing: 'owner'`, so the rule holds whether or not the backfill has run. The invite form drops the option when it is taken, and **the server refuses it regardless** — React hiding is not enforcement **2026-08-29, a pending invitation can be withdrawn:** `DELETE /api/companies/employees/invitations/:membershipId` moves an unclaimed seat `invited → inactive`. **The row is kept as history, never deleted.** The filter carries `company`, `status: 'invited'` **and** `user: null`, so a claimed or approved membership can never be removed through this route, and another company's seat answers 404 rather than disclosing that it exists. A withdrawn seat no longer matches a self-registration, and the screen stops listing it because the **server's own list** no longer carries it as open |
| `refreshtokens` | One row per issued Refresh Token, so a long-lived credential stays revocable | **The only server-side session state**, added 2026-08-27 — the collection the single-JWT design said would never exist. Stores a SHA-256 hash, never the token. Rotated on every use; a replay revokes the whole `family`. TTL index on `expiresAt` |
| `passwordresettokens` | One row per issued password-reset link, so a temporary credential stays revocable and single-use | **Added 2026-08-29** with the password-reset flow. Mirrors [[refreshtokens]] because it is the same kind of object: a SHA-256 hash, never the token; a **30-minute** expiry; `usedAt` for one-time use and `invalidatedAt` for a link a newer request retired; TTL index on `expiresAt`. **It is a collection rather than the two fields `docs/database-design.html` designs on [[users]]** — `security.resetTokenHash` + `security.resetTokenExpiresAt` cannot tell a spent link from an absent one, cannot record that a newer request replaced an older one, and put security state on the permanent identity document. That divergence is real and is recorded in [§8](#8-known-issues--risks) |
| `workentries` | One Completed Work entry on a contractor's profile — the tiles My Profile shows and Edit Profile manages | **Added 2026-08-29** with the profile backend, and it is **one of the two shapes [D13](#7-open-decisions) names**; the storage half of that decision is still formally open, so this row records what was built rather than claiming the decision. **Neither link is required** — `project` and `task` are optional by rule, so a free-standing portfolio entry, including work predating the account, is a complete entry and not a degraded one. **`fieldSyncVerifiedAt` is the `Completed on FieldSync` badge and is server-derived**: there is no field for it anywhere in the request contract, so a client cannot claim it. Absent means not verified; a timestamp means the backend proved the completed FieldSync relationship. Optional `image` → [[fileassets]], deleted with the entry. `owner` is in the filter of every query, so another person's entry is not refused — it is not found |
| `connections` | The request → accept graph between users | Gates messaging. Junction with a sorted `pair` + unique index (`connection_pair_unique`) = one edge per pair in either direction **Built 2026-08-29 (Browse backend), and [D17](#7-open-decisions) closed with it.** `status` is `pending | accepted | declined | removed | withdrawn`. **A teardown reuses the one pair row, it never adds a second**: `accepted → removed`, `pending outgoing → withdrawn`, and a refusal is `declined`. **`declined` is historical, not permanent** — it is one of the three reactivatable states, so the same pair may ask again. A fresh request **rewrites `requester`/`recipient` to the new direction**, sets `pending`, refreshes `requestedAt` and `$unset`s `respondedAt`, so stale response metadata cannot linger. **Declining creates no Block** — [[blocks]] is the explicit permanent mechanism. Browse projects only four states (`none`, `outgoing_request`, `incoming_request`, `connected`); `declined`/`removed`/`withdrawn` all read as `none`, and **blocked is never among them** because a blocked person is not in the result set at all |
| `ratings` | One peer score per shared **completed** task | Gate enforced by a unique index **Security rule, owner 2026-08-29: a user may never rate themself.** The backend **rejects** any rating request where `raterUserId === ratedUserId`; **hiding the control in the UI is not enforcement** — the same reasoning that keeps `fieldSyncVerifiedAt` server-derived on [[workentries]] ~~feeds `users.ratingSummary`~~ — **corrected 2026-08-29: no `ratingSummary` field was built.** A summary is read on demand by `summaryForMany` / `summaryFor`, so there is no denormalised copy to go stale ~~no ratings endpoint exists yet~~ — **superseded 2026-08-29 (Browse backend): the domain is built.** `POST /api/ratings` exists, the self-check runs **before** any other work, and a unique index on `{ rater, ratee, task }` is what enforces one score per shared task rather than a read-then-write. **Eligibility is delegated to a `RatingEligibilityPort`**, and the only implementation today answers **`false` for everyone** because the Tasks / Completed Work domain that would prove a shared completed task does not exist. That is an **implementation dependency, not an open product decision** — the rule is decided, the evidence source is not built |
| `blocks` | One document per *blocker → blocked* pair — a person one user has chosen never to see or be seen by | **Added 2026-08-29 (Browse backend), closing [D19](#7-open-decisions).** It is a **collection of its own, not a `connections.status` value**: a block needs no connection to exist, and storing it as a connection state would make "we were connected" and "I blocked a stranger" the same row. Fields are `blockerUserId`, `blockedUserId`, `createdAt` — **the two sides stay distinct internally**, which is the whole of what D19 asked, so the platform always knows who acted. `blocker_blocked_unique` on `{ blockerUserId, blockedUserId }` makes a repeat block a no-op; a second index on `blockedUserId` serves the reverse lookup. **The effect is mutual and one read resolves it**: `findHiddenUserIds` runs a single `$or` over both fields, so A blocking B removes each from the other's Browse results and from each other's public profile (404, the same answer a stranger's missing id gets — existence is never disclosed). **Direction is never exposed to the other side**: nothing tells B that A blocked them |
| `projects` | One construction project: name, structured place, the date window, and **the company that owns it** | **Built 2026-08-30 (`feature/projects.backend`).** **The company is the owning context, never a person** — `company` + `createdBy`, where `createdBy` is provenance and grants nothing; the earlier `owner: User` design is superseded, and no second GC-membership definition of ownership exists to disagree with it. **Dates follow the closed model**: `startDate` + `targetEndDate` + `originalTargetEndDate` (never rewritten) + `overrunAllowanceDays` (`x`, set once by the יזם and **immutable** — an edit is refused, not ignored). `originalTargetEndDate + x` is a ceiling the target may never pass, and every move appends to `targetChanges[]` with the **actual overrun measured from the original**. Dates are UTC midnight from `YYYY-MM-DD`, so no timezone moves them a day. **There is no stored status.** It is derived on read from `startedAt` / `completedAt` / `pausedAt` — `planned → active → paused → completed` — because the closed rule is that statuses follow the working schedule automatically; the old `planning\|active\|completed\|archived` enum is gone and no API can set one. **Cancellation deletes the row** (D24: *נעלם כאילו לא היה*), pre-start only. `location` reuses the shared structured place and `$unset`s whole on an explicit clear, so no legacy city survives underneath a removed place |
| `profilereminderdismissals` | One document per user: they dismissed the Personal dashboard's profile reminder, and **exactly which gaps** they were shown at the time | **Added 2026-08-29 (Personal dashboard backend).** Fields are `user` (unique), `version`, `dismissedKeys[]`, `dismissedAt`. **It stores the dismissed gaps, not a boolean and not a date.** A boolean silences the reminder for ever, including gaps that appear later; a date is a nag on a timer. The key set makes the rule exact — the reminder stays hidden while the current gaps are a **subset** of what was dismissed, and returns the moment a gap appears that the person never dismissed. **Its own collection, not a field on `users`**: whether somebody silenced one piece of dashboard advice is not part of their identity, and a person who never dismisses correctly has no row at all. **Dismissal hides the reminder; it never fakes completeness** — the API still reports the real missing list beside `visible: false`. **Not [D18](#7-open-decisions)**, which asks where a dismissed *cleanup item* is remembered and stays open |
| `projects` | Container for tasks; has an owner | Deliberately thin — members and tasks live in their own collections |
| `memberships` | A `user × project` link holding the **per-project role** | Role lives here, *not* on the user. ~~Also the write-side authorisation table~~ — **superseded 2026-08-30, see `projectmemberships` below** |
| `projectmemberships` | One person's participation in one project, **and what they may do there** | **Built 2026-08-30 (`feature/project-members.backend`).** The designed `memberships` row above, as implemented — and it differs in the one way that matters: **the role stopped granting anything.** The document carries `projectRole` (descriptive, `main_contractor \| subcontractor \| professional \| supplier \| viewer`, **never read by any check**) beside `permissions[]` and `fullAuthority`, which are the only two fields authority comes from. `fullAuthority` is a **flag, never an expanded copy of the list**, so a permission added to the scope later is covered without re-granting — which is what makes the approved disclaimer literally true. `status` is `invited \| active \| declined \| removed`: **`accepted` was renamed `active`** for consistency, and **`declined` is its own state and is not permanent** — the same person may be invited again, reusing the one row, exactly as [D17](#7-open-decisions) closed it for connection requests. Reusing `removed` for a refusal was rejected: being removed and saying no are different facts. `company` is **attribution, never authority** — the business the participant acts through, read by nothing. `invitedBy` is provenance and is **never inherited**. `{ project, user }` is unique, so re-inviting edits rather than duplicating. **The same row is called a membership by Project members and a grant by Permissions** — one model, two views, no second permission system |
| `permissiontemplates` | A named, reusable set of project permissions, owned by a company | **Built 2026-08-30.** Applying one **copies it at that moment**; a grant never points at a template, so editing the template later changes nothing anybody already holds. Same rule as `copy permissions from …`, and for the same reason: a person's authority must be readable from their own row alone. `{ company, name }` unique — one company's vocabulary never leaks into another's |
| `companycalendarversions` | One **frozen** version of a company's default working calendar | **Built 2026-08-30**, and it is the storage half of [D22](#7-open-decisions), whose behaviour was already closed. **Versions are never edited** — changing the company default appends a new one, and `projects.calendarVersion` pins a version by id. That pin is the whole safety mechanism: somebody editing the company default cannot move dates inside a running project, because the project keeps pointing at a document that keeps saying what it always said. `{ company, version }` unique makes the numbering safe under concurrency — two simultaneous edits cannot both claim v4; the loser retries against the version that won |
| `tasks` | One unit of work — project work or standalone work, in ONE collection | **Built 2026-08-30 (`feature/my-tasks.backend`).** **Owner decision on [D5](#7-open-decisions): the source is a STORED `kind`** (`project \| standalone`) beside an optional `project` reference — the earlier `project: null` shape was *modelled, not approved*, and the stored kind is now the answer rather than a conclusion drawn from an empty field. A pre-validate hook refuses the two ways they could disagree. **There is no stored status and no stored `overdue`**: Start and Complete write `startedAt` / `completedAt`, the three states are derived on read, and overdue is a separate comparison against `dueDate` — deliberately not one of the states, because work can be overdue in any of them. **There are NO task-to-task dependency edges** — the designed `dependencies: [ObjectId]` array is gone by owner decision; edges live between stages. `delegation` is embedded (`delegate`, `scope`, `partDescription?`, `delegatedAt`) because it is one fact written and cleared as one; `ownCrewOnly` and `delegatorOnSiteRequired` carry the GC's closed engagement terms. `assignee` becomes ABSENT on an orphan and `previousAssignee` preserves who it was, which the closed orphan rule requires |
| `projectstages` | One stage of one project's work, and the edges between stages | **Built 2026-08-30 by owner decision on the dependency representation**, which the Decision Map recorded as *"the representation itself… and it is the owner's"*. Dependencies run between **whole stages**, per the closed domain finding `אין דבר כזה בבניין עבודה ספציפית`; `isGate` flags a true gate; `dependsOn` holds directed edges that are **kept free of cycles** — an edge is refused when the stage it points at can already reach this one, checked against the graph as it *would* be, before anything is written. `order` and `dependsOn` are separate on purpose. `partialRelease*` records `שחרור חלקי` — **written by nothing yet: its permission representation is still open**, while its authority chain is closed. Every write above is governed by **`project.stage.manage`** (owner decision 2026-08-30), never by `project.edit`, and never by a role or title |
| `privateworkitems` | The receiver's own sub-tasks and notes on work they were given | **Built 2026-08-30.** Moved out of the embedded `subTasks` array the schema drew inside `tasks`: embedding put the private layer inside the document the serializer sends upward, so every read had to remember to strip it, while a separate owner-scoped collection means the wrong person's query returns nothing. **Delegation hides WHO performs; this hides HOW they organise it** — two features sharing a serializer and nothing else. A sub-task is a checklist item with no dependency edges, and **the public state is never derived from it**: the receiver sets that with Start and Complete, or the method leaks upward through the status field. `{ task, owner, order }` puts the owner IN the index, because reading the layer by task alone would be the leak |
| `fileassets` | Uploaded files: who owns them, what they are, and where the bytes live | **Built 2026-08-29** with the profile backend, and **[D1](#7-open-decisions) closed the same day**: the bytes go to **GridFS**, the driver is recorded as `storage.driver` and this row is the business record pointing at them. The 16 MB document cap is what makes inline bytes impossible for ~30 MB uploads. **What is built is narrower than the design:** `scope.type` is `avatar` or `work_entry` (task, project and message arrive with their stages), `scope.id` is nullable because an avatar has nothing to point at, and the allow-list is images only. **Versioning and `visibility: private` — the delegation-private upload — are designed and deliberately not built**; today every asset is reachable only by its owner, which is stricter than either `visibility` value. The lookup is by id **and** owner in one query, so knowing an asset id grants nothing |
| `rescheduleproposals` | The negotiation object; carries the proposal state machine | Embedded `items[]` = one atomic document, so concurrent responses cannot interleave. Semantics still D2 |
| `auditentries` | Append-only history of what changed and who changed it | `actor` is **always the delegator**, never the delegate. Snapshots the actor name so history survives soft-delete → D8 |
| `threads` | Direct (connection-gated), project, and GC-moderated conversations | Denormalised `lastMessageAt`/preview so the inbox is one query |
| `messages` | One document per message; attachments; agreement form → auto-task | The canonical never-embed case (unbounded + 16 MB cap) |
| `notifications` | Invitations, schedule changes, approvals/counters, etc. | Self-contained `payload` snapshot → no joins at render. Delivery method is D3 (no schema impact) |
| `mutes` | Contractor / project / conversation mutes | One collection, not three arrays, so Settings can list them all in one query |
| `reports` | User-filed reports for admin review | Snapshots the reported content — evidence must outlive it. Admin module (late stage) |
| `plans` | Seeded catalogue of subscription tiers: prices per currency and every numeric limit | Three documents (`free`/`basic`/`premium`). Codes only, no stored labels — the UI is bilingual. All limits carry `provisional: true` |
| `subscriptions` | One document per subscription period a user has held; the `active` one is current | Sale is snapshotted (`planCode`, `amountMinor`, `currency`), never re-read from the catalogue. A **partial unique** index allows exactly one active row per user |
| `usagecounters` | Metered allowance per user × metric × week — today only `cascade_simulation` | Quota enforced inside the update filter, so two concurrent requests cannot both spend the last unit. Unlimited tiers write nothing |
| `rules` | Personal automation rules | **Stretch only** — deliberately referenced by nothing else, so it can be cut without a migration |

### 3.3 Delegation visibility model (depth = 1)

> **Decided (D7, 2026-06-02): single-level delegation only — no re-delegation.** A delegate cannot
> delegate the work onward. If a delegate can't do it, the **original assignee** (the delegator) simply
> picks a different delegate. This keeps the privacy model to exactly **one hop in each direction**.

**The visibility triangle.** For a task the GC assigned to a delegator, who then delegated to a delegate:

```
   GC  ◄──sees──  Delegator  ◄──sees──  Delegate
   (party above)              (chose them)      (knows who they work for)

   GC  ──✗ does NOT see ✗──►  Delegate      (the core privacy guarantee)
   Delegate  ──✗ does NOT see ✗──►  GC       (work came from the delegator, full stop)
```

- **GC (the party above) sees only the delegator.** The delegate is invisible to them. This is the
  load-bearing guarantee — the GC believes the delegator is doing the work.
- **Delegator sees the delegate.** They chose them; the delegate is in their collaborator list; they're
  managing the relationship.
- **Delegate sees the delegator, not the GC.** From the delegate's side the work came from the delegator
  and that's the only relationship that exists.

**Constraints this forces (the serializer must implement all of these):**

1. **Task `assignee` stays the delegator on the GC-facing view.** The GC's contract of record is with the
   delegator. The `delegate` is a separate field visible only to the delegator-and-down — never serialized
   toward the party above. *(Updates the `Task` entity note in §3.2.)*
2. **Invisibility is symmetric, so it extends past the single task to the whole project context.** Because
   the delegate is invisible to the GC, the reverse must also hold or it's a one-way leak: the delegate must
   **not** see the project's other members, the ownership of other tasks in the DAG, or the project audit
   log — those would expose the GC and the wider project the delegate was never introduced to. The delegate
   sees a narrow slice: their task, its dates, its files, and the delegator. **Open sub-question** (parked):
   when the delegate views their task's *dependencies*, do they see the upstream **dates** (needed to know
   when they can start) while the upstream **owners** stay hidden? Leaning yes — dates visible, owners not —
   but confirm at Stage 5.
3. **The cascade/negotiation runs against the delegator, never the delegate.** This follows from the spec's
   "private execution layer" + "GC-visible status set manually," now sharpened by D7. There are two layers:
   - **Public layer — GC ↔ Delegator:** appears in the project, logged in the project audit, drives the
     *delegator's* public flexibility score. The GC only ever sees the delegator proposing/approving.
   - **Private layer — Delegator ↔ Delegate:** the delegate's real start/complete and any "I'll be late"
     live here, invisible to the GC. The delegator decides whether to absorb the slip or pass it up to the
     GC as a reschedule request, and **manually** sets the GC-visible status.
4. **Audit log names the delegator as the actor**, never the delegate (the serializer applied to audit entries).
5. **The feature may not be advertised on any surface a non-delegate can read.** The four constraints
   above govern what the *serializer* returns and say nothing about what the *product copy* says — and
   copy is a leak path no serializer can close. A page that announces confidential delegation tells
   every GC on the platform that the mechanism exists, that the sub they contracted with can use it,
   and that it is built to be invisible to them specifically; that is the guarantee described to the
   one party it is kept from. So **no marketing block, feature list, tooltip, empty state or help text
   on a signed-out or GC-reachable surface may name delegation, describe its mechanics, or state that
   it is confidential** — and the repair is never a gentler explanation of the same mechanism, because
   any accurate description of it is the leak. Applied to `login.html` on 2026-08-25 (`aedca18`)
   and to `landing.html` the same day (`3a9fd52`); **no surface in the project names the feature
   today.** **This constraint closes a leak and opens a problem in the same move:** a contractor who
   may legitimately delegate still has to find out that they can, and nothing anywhere tells them.
   That is [D26](#7-open-decisions), and it is open — the constraint is satisfied at the cost of a
   capability that is currently undiscoverable.

**Addendum (validated 2026-08-28) — departures, whole-or-part, and what happens on site.** Three
rules the model owns and this section did not carry.

**(a) Departures are asymmetric, and the asymmetry is the point.** If the **delegate** leaves,
responsibility **returns automatically to the delegator** — `האחריות חוזרת אוטומטית ל-delegator` —
who either performs the work himself or brings another; **the GC never learns anything changed**, so
the triangle survives the departure untouched. If the **delegator** leaves, there are two paths and
he chooses between them: the **voluntary handoff**, where he himself tells the GC to work directly
with the delegate and, **if the GC accepts, the delegate is appointed responsible for the work**; or
he takes the delegate out with him. **Permanent confidentiality is not weakened by this.** The
relationship surfaces only at that handoff, and only because the delegator disclosed it — it is
**his** disclosure, never the platform's, and nothing here lets the system reveal a delegate.

**(b) Whole-or-part is the delegator's choice.** `delegation יכול להיות לעבודה שלמה או רק לחלק
מהעבודה, זאת בחירה של הקבלן שמעביר את העבודה`. Part-delegation is real, which closes the
sub-task-granularity question the private layer had been carrying: **the delegated part rides the
existing private sub-task model**, and nothing new is invented for it. The rest of the closed model
is unchanged — single level only, gated on the GC's general acceptance of the contractor he closed
with, the GC may demand own-crew-only or require the delegator on site, the delegator remains the
**sole responsible party and the one who appears as performer**, only the delegator has access to
who actually performs, and the delegate knows upfront he is the delegator's subcontractor.

**(c) On-site exposure is a real-world fact, not a platform behaviour.** On the site itself a GC,
`מנהל עבודה` or `מנהל אתרים` may meet whoever is actually working and be told `אני מפעיל של x` —
that is people talking on a building site. **It changes nothing about the guarantees above:** the
serializer still never returns the delegate, and **constraint 5 must never be "corrected" against
this** — that someone can learn a fact in person is not a reason for the product to state it.

**Note for D6 (flexibility formula):** D7 means a contractor accrues reschedule behaviour in
*two* roles — as a delegator negotiating up toward a GC, and as a delegate negotiating toward a delegator.

**Attribution is now closed (2026-08-29).** When work is delegated, **the delegator remains the party
responsible toward the project**, so any Flexibility Score impact arising from that project commitment
belongs to **the delegator, not the delegate**. This follows the model above rather than adding to it:
the serializer never returns the delegate, [[auditentries]]`.actor` is *always the delegator*, and the
GC negotiates with the delegator throughout — a score that landed on a party the project cannot see,
and did not contract with, would be attributing a commitment to somebody who never made it.

~~Whether those roll into one score or stay contextual is part of D6, now with this wrinkle attached.~~
What remains open in D6 is **arithmetic only** — attribution is no longer part of it.

### 3.4 Bilingual & bidirectional UI (Hebrew RTL default · English LTR)

> **A product requirement, not a feature.** Every screen, every label, every error, every notification
> exists in **both** Hebrew and English. Hebrew is the default. This is cross-cutting: it is not a row in
> the roadmap that can be cut, it is a constraint on every row.

**Two axes that must not be confused.** *Language* is which strings appear; *direction* is which way the
layout flows. They happen to move together here, but they are handled by different mechanisms — swapping
strings does not mirror a layout, and mirroring a layout does not translate anything.

| | Hebrew (default) | English |
|---|---|---|
| `lang` | `he` | `en` |
| `dir` | `rtl` | `ltr` |
| Sans | Heebo | Inter |
| Serif | Frank Ruhl Libre | Fraunces |
| Mono | IBM Plex Mono (both) | IBM Plex Mono (both) |

**Rules for every screen from here on:**

1. **Direction lives on `<html>` and nowhere else.** `<html lang="he" dir="rtl">` ⇄ `<html lang="en" dir="ltr">`.
   Everything below inherits. *Current debt, now partly paid:* most static prototypes hard-code
   `<html lang="he">` and flip `direction: ltr` on individual containers instead — so a screen reader
   announces the English text as Hebrew. **The three auth screens stopped doing that on 2026-08-25
   (`c6d53c5`):** `login.css` sets `direction` on `html` and flips it with `html:has(#lang-en:checked)`, and
   `lang.ts` syncs `<html lang>` and `<html dir>` to the chosen language. Every other screen is still
   tracked in [§8](#8-known-issues--risks); the rest resolves in the React migration.
2. **Logical CSS properties only — never `left`/`right`.** Use `margin-inline-start`, `padding-inline-end`,
   `inset-inline-start`, `border-inline-start`, `text-align: start` / `end`. A layout built from logical
   properties mirrors itself for free. A single hard-coded `left` is a bug that is invisible in one
   language and obvious in the other.
3. **Mirror what is directional; leave everything else alone.** Mirror: arrows, chevrons, back/next
   buttons, progress-bar fill, the timeline and the dependency-DAG flow direction. Do **not** mirror:
   logos, avatars, photos, the hard-hat warning icon, clocks, phone numbers, or media controls.
4. **`dir="auto"` on every field that renders user-generated content.** A bio, a task title, a project
   name, a message body — these are in whatever language the person typed, which is **not** necessarily
   the UI language. A Hebrew message inside an English UI renders with its punctuation in the wrong place
   unless the browser is allowed to decide per string.
5. **Isolate mixed-direction runs.** Hebrew text containing Latin digits, a model number or a brand name
   is bidirectional *within a single string*. Wrap the embedded run in `<bdi>` (or `unicode-bidi: isolate`)
   so the surrounding text can't reorder it. Format dates and numbers with `Intl.DateTimeFormat` /
   `Intl.NumberFormat`, never by concatenating strings.
6. **Both languages are checked before anything is Done** — see the definition of Done above.
7. **Never put `dir="auto"` on an element that holds both `.he-text` and `.en-text`.** This is specific to
   the duplicate-markup prototypes and it fails silently. `display: none` does **not** remove text from the
   bidi algorithm, so `dir="auto"` on a shared parent resolves from the first strong character — always the
   Hebrew — and the element renders **RTL even in English**. Put `dir="auto"` on each single-language span
   instead. Under React, where one string exists per render, it moves back onto the element. Cost of getting
   it wrong: the English screen looks like a separately composed layout. See [§10](#10-bug--issue-log).

8. **Hebrew copy addresses ONE person, and plural is never a gender workaround** (owner rule, 2026-08-29). The product
   speaks to a single contractor, so forms such as `בחרו`, `הזינו`, `אישרתם`, `הסרתם` and `ברצונכם` are wrong even
   though they dodge grammatical gender, and **slash forms such as `בחר/י` are not permitted without approval**. The
   answer is to phrase it as a noun or impersonally, which is gender-free without being plural: `בחירת מיקום` rather
   than `בחרו מיקום`, `הזנת פרטים` rather than `הזינו פרטים`, `המקומות שהוסרו` rather than `המקומות שהסרתם`. **Plural is
   correct only when the text genuinely addresses more than one person.** **English is singular too, and keeps its
   natural voice** — English "you" *is* singular, so `Your projects are waiting` needs no repair; this rule is about
   Hebrew's plural-as-a-workaround, not about removing the second person from English. Applied across every migrated
   screen on 2026-08-29; the exception hunt is [§10](#10-bug--issue-log).

**Data-model consequences.** Bilingualism is not only a UI concern; it constrains what may be *stored*.
All of this is reflected in [`docs/database-design.html`](docs/database-design.html):

- **`users.language`** (`'he' | 'en'`, default `'he'`). `localStorage` is per-device — a contractor who
  switches to English on the site-office desktop should not be back in Hebrew on their phone. localStorage
  stays as the pre-login default only.
- **Enums are stored as language-neutral codes.** Trades, regions, roles, statuses, notification types and
  audit actions are stable ASCII keys (`'concrete'`, `'gc'`, `'not_started'`) translated at render time.
  Storing the Hebrew label would make the English UI impossible *and* would invalidate every index the
  first time a translation is corrected.
- **Nothing user-visible is stored pre-rendered.** `notifications.payload` holds the *parts*
  (`taskTitle`, `proposerName`, dates) and the client composes the sentence from `type`. Otherwise a
  notification created in Hebrew stays Hebrew forever, including after the user switches to English.
  Same reasoning for `auditentries`: an `action` enum plus a `changes` diff, composed client-side.
- **The API returns error *codes*, not error *messages*.** `{ error: 'INVALID_CREDENTIALS' }`, translated
  by the client. Otherwise the unified login error only exists in one language.
- **Sorting Hebrew needs a collation.** MongoDB sorts byte-wise by default, which is not alphabetical for
  Hebrew. Sorting contractors by name uses a MongoDB collation (`{ locale: 'he' }`) or `Intl.Collator`
  client-side. Rating and flexibility sort numerically and are unaffected.

**Migration path — the current approach does not scale.** The static prototypes duplicate every string as
`.he-text` / `.en-text` and swap them with a hidden-radio CSS toggle, because that is the only way to do it
with no JavaScript. The cost is already visible on the register screen: the DOM carries every string twice,
the trade `<select>` had to be duplicated wholesale (option text can't use the span trick), and the form
needs `novalidate` because the hidden inactive-language inputs would otherwise block submit. Across 36
screens this is unmaintainable. **Under React it becomes one set of components plus a translation resource
file**, with the active language in Context and `dir` set on `<html>`; the duplicate-markup pattern is
dropped entirely. Exactly how is [D10](#7-open-decisions).

### 3.5 Shared UI rules (apply to every screen)

> Set on 2026-08-19 after a review found the same three regressions on more than one screen.
> These are **project-wide**, not per-screen preferences. Each one fails quietly, which is why it
> is written down rather than left to review.

1. **Focus must not move anything.** A field may change colour, border or shadow on `:hover` and
   `:focus`, but it must not change position or size. `transform: translateY(-1px)` on a field was
   the actual cause of the "placeholder jumps on focus" report — the whole control shifted, taking
   the placeholder with it. Removed from `edit-profile.css`, `register.css` and
   `browse-contractors.css`. Button transforms are fine: a button is not a text field.
2. **No floating labels.** A placeholder stays in place until the user types. There is no
   floating-label pattern anywhere in the project and none may be introduced without approval.
   *(Verified: no screen used one — the reported symptom was rule 1.)*
3. **Labels and identifying metadata stay readable.** A label such as City, Phone, Region, Work area
   or Specialty is information the user is expected to read, so it may not shrink into micro-text.
   **`register.css` at 14px is the reference size.** Corrected to match: `profile.css` (11.5px),
   `browse-contractors.css` (11px), `login.css` (11px). Labels need not equal body size; they must
   stay comfortably legible — many contractors using this are older users.
   **That 2026-08-19 pass was not exhaustive, which is the part worth remembering.** It fixed the
   `.form-label` controls it went looking for and left three *card* values behind on the same screen:
   the location row at 13px, the flexibility label at 12.5px and the connection badge at 11px, all
   corrected on 2026-08-20 (`ede1c52`). The rule covers identifying metadata **wherever it appears**,
   not only text sitting above a form field — so a screen is not "corrected" until its cards, badges
   and rows have been measured too, against the equivalent element on every screen that shows the
   same information.
4. **Secondary text must still clear contrast.** Secondary information stays visually secondary, but it
   has to be readable. Two tiers only, both measured against the cream card surfaces: **`--text-mute`
   (0.64 alpha, ≈4.6:1)** for metadata — dates, scales, counters, empty states — and **`--text-soft`
   (0.75, ≈6.8:1)** for hints, ledes, labels and secondary body copy. The tokens are named by role, not by
   opacity, because the numbers moved once. *Why only two tiers:* the old scale had four (0.40 / 0.55 /
   0.75 / 1.0), and the first two measured **2.4:1 and 3.6:1** — both below AA. Lifting them above the
   4.5:1 floor leaves no visual room for a third secondary step, so 0.55 and 0.75 merged. Losing that
   distinction is the honest cost of making the text legible.
5. **Let text wrap to the space it has.** No manual line breaks, and no width limit tighter than the
   content needs. A reading measure (`--prose-max`) is kept only for genuinely long prose — a bio, a
   long answer — never for a label, a hint or a one-line section lede. Applies in both directions.
6. **Supporting text has a floor, and it is 15px.** Secondary copy may stay visually secondary — by
   colour, by a divider, by position — but never by being small enough that a reader has to work at
   it. **The project reference is `register.css`, whose `.brand-footer-note` is 15px** (its field
   hints are 14.5px); `login.css` carried that same class at **12px** until 2026-08-25. An
   intermediate **13.5px was proposed and is not an approved size** — it was still under the floor.
   This is the third instance of one failure, after the 2026-08-19 label sweep and the 2026-08-20
   card-value pass, which is why the floor is now a number instead of a judgement.

### 3.6 Employee Management — authorization, visibility and session state

> Added 2026-08-29 with the Employee Management + onboarding build. **Implemented on
> `feature/employee-management-react` (`3ceab43`) and `feature/employee-management-onboarding.backend`
> (`c1a4c5a`), and NOT merged into `develop`.** Implemented is not integrated.

**1. Authorization is the canonical capability, and nothing else.** Every Employee Management action —
opening a seat, listing memberships, approving one, approving all, recording employee setup — is
authorised by the caller's own recorded **`company.invite_employees`** permission on an **active**
membership. It is **never** inferred from `standing`, from `companyPosition` or from a job title, and
the client contains no rule of the shape *if standing is owner, allow everything*. **The backend
remains the security boundary**: the client makes the request and renders the server's own 403.

**2. UI visibility may use canonical state; it is not authorization.** The frontend may hide controls
the server would refuse, because a control that can never succeed is worse than an absent one. Hiding
is a courtesy and changes no guarantee — the API refuses either way.

**3. A user whose `standing` is `employee` sees neither entry point.** Not the Employee Management
navbar item, and not the Personal dashboard Management section — and **no empty Management section is
rendered for them either**. This is a **UI visibility rule only**; actual authority is still the
capability in rule 1.

**4. Employee Management is a permanent authenticated destination.** It is reachable from the
authenticated navbar and from the Personal dashboard Management section, for eligible management
users, **for as long as they hold the capability**. Completing the optional onboarding step does not
remove it and **skipping it does not remove it** — skipping declines the first-run experience, not
the feature.

**5. Current company comes from the session, and an ended relationship is not current.** Login and
`GET /api/auth/me` answer the same `SessionUser`, whose `company` is a `CompanyContext` carrying
`id`, `standing`, `membershipStatus`, `permissions` and `employeeSetupComplete` — and deliberately no
company name, office phone or availability, which belong to the profile feature.

| Membership the person holds | `company` on the session payload |
|---|---|
| none | `null` |
| one `pending_company_approval` | that context — what the Waiting for Approval screen runs on |
| one `active` | that context — normal access |
| `inactive` / ended only | `null` |

`membershipStatus` is carried as the **code** it is rather than reduced to a boolean, because waiting
for approval and having left are different facts that no boolean separates. **An `inactive` membership
is history and is never reported as the current company.**

**6. Historical permissions are stored and are never current capabilities.** When a membership becomes
`inactive` its `permissions` array is **preserved for history and audit** and is **not** automatically
cleared — an audit trail that erases what somebody was allowed to do cannot answer what they were
allowed to do. Being stored, it is **not** returned as a current capability, **not** exposed through
`CompanyContext`, grants **no** Employee Management access, grants **no** company or project
authority, and affects **no** routing. **Stored historically ≠ currently authorized**, and the read is
what enforces it: an ended row never becomes a company context, so its permissions have nowhere to
reach.

**7. Employee Management is not Permissions.** Approval moves `pending_company_approval` → `active`
and grants nothing — no company permissions, no project permissions, no copy of the owner's set, and
nothing inferred from `companyPosition` or a job title. **Permissions stays a separate future feature
and is not built.**

**8. The canonical `companyPosition` vocabulary.** One label per code per language, and this table is
the single source. Employee Management's invitation form and Register both render from it; neither
holds a second mapping.

| Code | Hebrew | English |
|---|---|---|
| `main_contractor` | **`קבלן ביצוע ראשי`** | Main Contractor |
| `construction_manager` | `מנהל אתרים` | Construction Manager |
| `regional_construction_manager` | `מנהל אתרים אזורי` | Regional Construction Manager |
| `site_manager` | `מנהל עבודה` | Site Manager |
| `contractor` | `קבלן` | Contractor |
| `employee` | `עובד` | Employee |

**`main_contractor` is `קבלן ביצוע ראשי`, approved 2026-08-29, replacing `קבלן ראשי`.** The English
label does **not** change. The reason the Hebrew moved is a genuine collision: `קבלן ראשי` is already
the Hebrew for the **per-project role** `gc` on [[memberships]], and a job title on a company
relationship and a role on a project are different things that must not share a string. **`קבלן ראשי`
is therefore still correct where it labels the project role** — including the My projects role codes
in [§8](#8-known-issues--risks) — and is no longer the label for this code.

**`מנהל אתר` is not a label.** It is a synonym of `מנהל עבודה` and is deliberately **not** a second
primary label for `site_manager`: a product that calls one role two things has two roles as far as a
reader is concerned. Keep it for a glossary or a search alias if one is ever needed.

**The codes are stable identifiers and are never renamed to follow a label** — `main_contractor` did
not move when its Hebrew did, exactly as `site_manager` did not when its Hebrew was reworded twice.

---

### 3.7 Static prototypes, and the environment conventions the client depends on

**The static screens under `Frontend/screens/` are TEMPORARY OBSOLETE MIGRATION REFERENCES.** They are not the source of
truth for anything. Where a static screen and the integrated React screen disagree, **the React screen is current** and the
static file is stale — it predates decisions that superseded it.

**They serve a migration process, not only a side-by-side comparison.** For each screen the process is: audit the
prototype for what it actually requires, reconcile that against the newer approved decisions, build the React screen,
integrate it into `develop`, check that nothing approved was left behind only in the static file, and *then* let the
owner compare the two during manual QA. A prototype is retired when that whole process has run for it — not when it
merely looks similar to a React screen. Nothing in them is a product rule, and three concrete traps are recorded:

- they **hard-code a person's name in two languages** (`בר כאכון` / `Bar Kakon`) and swap them with the language, which is
  the opposite of the rule that a person's name is user data ([§10](#10-bug--issue-log));
- they carry a **Minimum flexibility** control for a number the product cannot compute ([D6](#7-open-decisions));
- they use **free-text city boxes**, superseded by the shared structured-location module.

**Deleted 2026-08-29, by owner approval, each one only after that process had run for it** — React replacement
integrated in `develop`, reachable on a real route, and checked for stranded requirements: `register.html` + `.css` ·
`login.html` + `.css` · `forgot-password.html` · `reset-password.html` · `my-profile.html` + `.css` ·
`edit-profile.html` + `.css` · `browse-contractors.html` + `.css` · `profile.css` (the sheet only the two profile
prototypes loaded) · `validation.ts` / `validation.js` (loaded by five of the deleted screens and by nothing else).
`employee-management.html` was listed here in error and **never existed**. Each file remains in git history and on its
own feature branch, so deleting it removes a stale copy, not a record. **The check did find one stranded requirement —
Browse's unmigrated Sort control — and it is written up in [§8](#8-known-issues--risks) and being restored rather than
lost with the file.**

**Retained, because no React replacement exists in `develop`:** `my-network.html` + `my-network.css`,
`personal-dashboard.html` + `personal-dashboard.css`, `my-projects.html` + `my-projects.css`,
`create-project.html` + `create-project.css`, and the shared `lang.ts` / `lang.js` that they load.

**`my-projects` and `create-project` were reconciled in on 2026-08-30, the same way and for the same reason.**
Both existed **only** under the pre-reorganisation root `screens/`, on `feature/my-projects` (`a791231`) and
`feature/create-project` (`36c3d82`) — one commit each, touching nothing else, and **never migrated**: no ref
in the repository has ever held them under `Frontend/screens/`. Merging either branch would have recreated
the deleted root directory, so both were copied out instead. Each `.css` is **byte-identical**
(`6a68944`, `f6f9794`); each `.html` differs in exactly **two lines** — the navbar links to
`browse-contractors.html` and `my-tasks.html`, whose targets do not exist, repointed to `#`. Root `screens/`
was **not** recreated. A side effect worth naming: `personal-dashboard.html` and `my-network.html` had linked
to `my-projects.html` and `create-project.html` since they were written, and **those links resolve now for
the first time**.

**One dangling reference was created and deliberately not repaired:** `create-project.html` loads
`validation.js`, which was intentionally deleted from `develop` in `ced43ba` because only the five screens
deleted alongside it used one. It was **not reintroduced** — restoring a file that a cleanup removed on
purpose is a decision for the owner, not a side effect of a path fix. The consequence is limited to the
prototype: opening `create-project.html` directly logs one 404 and its CSS-only validation hints do not run.
Nothing in the React application references it.

**`personal-dashboard.html` arrived late, and how it arrived is the point (2026-08-29).** It was the one approved
prototype that had never been moved out of the pre-reorganisation **root `screens/`** directory: it existed only on
`feature/personal-dashboard`, whose two commits touch nothing else, and it was therefore invisible to every audit that
looked under `Frontend/screens/`. Merging that branch would have **recreated the deleted root `screens/`** — the exact
migration fault that Browse hit once already. It was reconciled instead: the two files were read out of the branch and
written to `Frontend/screens/`, the CSS **byte-identical** (`73552cd`), the HTML differing only in the three navbar
links whose targets no longer exist (`browse-contractors.html`, `my-projects.html`, `my-tasks.html` → `#`) — the same
treatment `my-network.html` received. Root `screens/` was **not** recreated. `my-network.html`'s long-dangling link to
`personal-dashboard.html` now resolves for the first time.

**The prototype is historical evidence, not a specification.** Its eight widgets read Tasks, Projects, reschedule
proposals, agreement forms and a notifications digest — **five domains the backend does not have** — so every number on
it is mock, and its Hebrew addresses a plural *you* (`ממתינות לכם`, `הצעתם`, `לאישורכם` — 15 occurrences) against the
[§3.x](#37-static-prototypes-and-the-environment-conventions-the-client-depends-on) single-user copy rule. None of that
may be carried into React.

**Still awaiting migration and never written as static here:** the landing page, my-projects, my-tasks,
create/edit-project, subscriptions and the error screens.

#### Google Maps Platform keys — two credentials, never one

| Where | Variable | Nature |
|---|---|---|
| `Frontend/.env` | **`VITE_GOOGLE_MAPS_API_KEY`** | A **browser** key. Anything prefixed `VITE_` is compiled into the bundle and is readable by anyone who opens the site — that is inherent, not a leak. It must be restricted by **HTTP referrer** and to **Places API (New)** only |
| `Backend/.env` | **`GOOGLE_MAPS_API_KEY`** | A **server** secret. Used for Routes (Compute Route Matrix) and server-side Places. Restricted by **IP**. Verified absent from the built frontend bundle |

They are **two different keys and must never hold the same value**. `.env` files are gitignored; the tracked `.env.example`
files carry **placeholders only**. The former alias **`VITE_GOOGLE_MAPS_BROWSER_KEY` is obsolete and absent** — one
canonical name. Both `.env.example` files had accumulated a **duplicate, undocumented stub** for their key beside the
documented block; both were cleaned up during integration. That is housekeeping, not a product rule.

## 4. Roadmap (stages)

Built so that **after Stage 3 we already have a complete, deployed, gradeable full-stack app** that
satisfies the syllabus. Stages 4–5 earn the "more than Trello" distinction. If time runs short, we cut
**from the back**.

1. **Foundations & walking skeleton** — repo structure, Express+Mongo, JWT scaffolding, one protected
   route, React+Vite app that logs in and hits it, error handler + JOI + Axios + PrivateRoute skeletons.
   **Deploy this thin slice immediately** to prove the pipeline.
2. **Identity & access** — full register/login, profile wizard, my/public profile, settings, browse
   contractors, network (request → accept → gate). Context (current user) + Redux store land here.
3. **Projects, membership, tasks — simple visibility only** — projects CRUD, membership/roles, tasks/jobs,
   binary start/complete, **stage-level** dependency editing **with cycle prevention** (never task-to-task —
   see the 2026-08-30 owner decision), dashboard (timeline + DAG view),
   Multer uploads. **No delegation yet.** ← *complete gradeable app at this point.*
4. **Cascade & negotiation** — naive delta-propagation, reschedule proposal + before/after review,
   approve/reject/counter state machine, audit log, flexibility score on resolution, schedule notifications.
5. **Dual-visibility privacy & delegation** — centralized viewer-aware serializer, private execution layer,
   invisible delegation, receiver-only classification, manual GC-visible status. Heavy testing.
6. **Communication & polish** — inbox (direct/project/GC-moderated), attachments, agreement-form →
   auto-task, full notifications, mute controls, performance pass (lazy loading + memoization).
7. **Admin & moderation, then rules engine (stretch)** — trim from here first if time is tight.

---

It answers `201 { user }` and Login is the only authenticator. The endpoint also gained `standing`, so it creates an owner *or* claims an employee's invitation

A turned-away visitor's destination travels in `location.state`, so Login returns them to it rather than always landing them on the dashboard. **Why it was deferred, kept because the reasoning still reads:** it was not required while Register was the only migrated screen and had nowhere to go; [D30](#7-open-decisions) closed 2026-08-29 with `Register → Login → Personal dashboard`, which names three destinations and so needs routing between them
- [ ] Client: minimal login that hits the protected route **end-to-end** — **still half done, and the missing half is now smaller than it was.** Login is end to end (`0911948`), and protected routes are real: `GET /api/health-auth` and the four `/api/companies/employees` routes all run through `requireAccessToken`. What no *screen* does yet is spend the token on one, because the screens that would (`/api/users/me`) still have no endpoint and the employee-management screen does not exist. So the token is proven issued, stored and **accepted** — by the verification scripts, not by the client
- [ ] Client: Redux store skeleton + Context skeleton (current user) — **the Context half exists** (`src/auth/AuthContext.tsx`, `0911948`): it holds the authenticated user Login returns, wraps the existing token storage rather than replacing it, and answers `isAuthenticated` for `PrivateRoute`. Redux is untouched, and nothing has needed it yet

> 36-screen list; many "screens" are popups/components, not routes.)

| Feature | Stage | Status | Notes |
