    is no delete route, so each run adds accounts. Harmless, anchored to obvious test addresses, and worth knowing
    before reading a Browse page during manual QA.
  - **Messaging does not exist**, so there is no Message control on a contractor card, and the rule that messaging must
    respect an active [[blocks]] row is written down but not yet enforceable anywhere.
  - **My network is still an unmigrated static screen**, so a card's *Manage in My network* control points nowhere.
  - **Create / Edit project must consume `Frontend/src/location/`** when it is migrated. Building a second Places
    implementation there would recreate exactly the duplication this integration removed.
  - **The owner backfill has not been run everywhere.** `migrate:owner-main-contractor` is idempotent and narrow, but rows
    created before the change still need it in any environment where it has not run.
  - **`.gitignore` lists `node_modules/` with a trailing slash**, which does not match a symlink of that name. It allowed
    symlinked `node_modules` to be committed twice during this work; both were caught and removed. A bare `node_modules`
    line would close it. Housekeeping, not product.

- **The shipped Hebrew label for `main_contractor` is now non-canonical, and the code is on an unmerged branch** (2026-08-29).
  The canonical Hebrew moved to **`קבלן ביצוע ראשי`** ([§3.6](#36-employee-management--authorization-visibility-and-session-state), rule 8),
  because the old `קבלן ראשי` collides with the Hebrew for the **per-project role** `gc` on [[memberships]] — a company job title and a
  project role must not share a string. **`Frontend/src/i18n/strings.he.ts` on `feature/employee-management-react` (`3ceab43`) still reads
  `main_contractor: 'קבלן ראשי'`.** It is one line in one file, the English label is unaffected, and the code `main_contractor` does not
  move — but it must be corrected **before that branch merges**, or the first integrated build ships the superseded label. It was not
  fixed in this pass because that pass was documentation-only and did not touch feature branches. **`קבלן ראשי` remains correct where it
  labels the project role**, so this is a one-line change and not a search-and-replace.

- **Employee Management + onboarding is implemented on two unmerged branches and has had no browser validation** (2026-08-29).
  `feature/employee-management-react` (`3ceab43`) and `feature/employee-management-onboarding.backend` (`c1a4c5a`). Backend behaviour is
  covered end to end by `npm run verify:employee-lifecycle` (49 checks) and the client's routing was exercised against the live API, but
  **nothing has been rendered in a browser**: Hebrew RTL, English LTR and the mobile/tablet/desktop ladder were verified **statically and
  at build level only**. Treat the visual layer as unproven until it is opened. Two further integration facts: the two branches are
  **independent**, so neither is runnable end to end from a single checkout until both merge, and
  **`npm run migrate:membership-uniqueness` must be run once per environment** or `user_current_unique` will be present in the code and
  absent from the database — see [§3.6](#36-employee-management--authorization-visibility-and-session-state).

- **The heavy-equipment picker has been on Edit Profile since 2026-08-19 and still saves nothing — it is now implementation debt, not only an open question** (2026-08-29).
  [D14](#7-open-decisions)'s equipment half has **no approved schema anywhere**, so the profile backend built on 2026-08-29 deliberately
  left it out: `PATCH /api/users/me` accepts no `equipment` field, [[users]] has no place to put one, and the ten-machine picker the
  screen renders is wired to nothing. **That was the correct call** — the alternative was inventing a shape mid-build, and this project
  does not invent schema — but the situation has changed in one respect. Until now the gap was a decision nobody had reached; now a
  screen the owner has approved offers a control that silently discards what the user selects. The three shapes named in D14 are
  unchanged and none has been chosen: a second array on [[users]] (`equipment: [String]`), a sub-document keyed by specialty, or a
  general "specialty attributes" mechanism other trades would reuse. **Decide it before Edit Profile is connected to the backend**, or
  the first user to pick a machine type will lose the selection on save with no error to explain it.

- **[[companies]] and [[companymemberships]] do not exist in `docs/database-design.html` at all**, and the profile pass deliberately did
  not add them (2026-08-29). Both collections have been **built since 2026-08-28** and the profile payload reads both of them — company
  name, office phone and availability come from [[companies]], and standing, position and the `company.manage` permission come from
  [[companymemberships]]. The schema page still has neither. **A partial entry written from what Profile happens to read would be worse
  than the omission:** it would document the two collections through the narrow slice one screen needs, and the next reader would trust
  it. These belong to the organizational / Employee Management schema pass, with the invitation lifecycle, the partial unique index and
  the permission model documented together. This item exists so that pass has a written reason to happen.

- **The profile backend is built and no screen is connected to it** (2026-08-29). `feature/my-profile.backend` (`5ecc46c`) implements
  every endpoint My Profile and Edit Profile need, and both React screens are still rendering the prototype's representative data. The
  feature is **not Done** and neither registry row says it is. Two things stand between here and Done: wiring the screens, and **a merge
  that has to happen first** — `feature/my-profile.backend` was branched from `develop`, whose backend predates all of the auth work, so
  it carries **no `passwordChangedAt` invalidation, no `users.status` enforcement on the Access Token path, no rate limiting and no
  employee lifecycle**. Those live on `feature/password-reset.backend`. `findActiveByUser` was written on the profile branch with the
  **identical signature** it has there, specifically so that merge is trivial, but the merge is real work and it is owed.

- ~~**`docs/database-design.html` silently hides two collections it documents**~~ ***(Resolved 2026-08-29, later the same day.)***
  ✅ **Both collections now render.** They were moved from the non-existent `core` domain to **`identity`**, which is where they already
  belonged: both hold credentials that prove who a user is, both reference [[users]] and nothing else, and `identity` is the domain
  [[users]] itself lives in. **No new domain was invented**, and nothing but the grouping changed — no field, no index, no relationship,
  and none of the auth semantics either collection documents. `passwordresettokens` also moved from `y: 470` to `y: 430`, which removes
  the 16px collision with [[usagecounters]] noted below; that collision was always in the data and was only invisible because the node
  was never drawn. The original report follows.
  (Found 2026-08-29 while adding [[workentries]]; the defect is older.) [[refreshtokens]] and [[passwordresettokens]] carried
  `domain: 'core'`, and `core` was not a key of the page's `DOMAINS` map. Both the collections sidebar and the ERD iterate the map's
  keys, so neither collection was listed, neither node was drawn, and selecting one by hand threw on `DOMAINS[c.domain].label`. Nothing
  warned; they were simply absent from a page whose whole job is completeness. **Not fixed in the profile pass**, which was scoped to the
  profile model and would have been the wrong place to change what the ERD draws.

- **The [D9](#7-open-decisions) reversal leaves user-visible copy stating a rule the product no longer has** (2026-08-29).
  ✅ **(a) is paid — the React Login carries the approved replacement (`0911948`); (b) `my-network.html` is untouched and still owed.**
  The owner approved the wording on 2026-08-29: `הודעה ראשונה ממי שאינכם מחוברים אליו מגיעה כבקשה — לאישור, לדחייה או לחסימה`
  / *A first message from someone you are not connected to arrives as a request — approve, decline, or block.*
  It states the request gate, names no delegation, and repeats neither bullet beside it — the three
  constraints this item set. **`screens/login.html` still carries the old sentence and was left alone**,
  because the static prototypes are the visual reference until the React screens are accepted; they are
  deleted together, and this item closes with them. The original report follows.
  The documentation was corrected in that pass; **no screen file was touched**, so two screens still
  teach the reversed gate. **(a) `screens/login.html` is the urgent one** — its brand panel reads
  `הודעות נפתחות רק לאחר אישור בקשת חיבור` / *Messaging opens only after a connection is accepted*
  on a **shipped, signed-out** surface, which makes it the only place the old gate is visible to a
  stranger. **The replacement wording is owner-approval copy and was deliberately not invented**; a
  faithful one-line statement of the request-gate model is the natural candidate, and whatever
  replaces it must still not name delegation ([D26](#7-open-decisions), [§3.3](#33-delegation-visibility-model-depth--1)
  constraint 5) and must not overlap the two bullets beside it. **(b) `screens/my-network.html`** —
  its group ledes narrate `request → accept → then message` and its removal dialog claims direct
  messaging closes. The ledes and the dialog may describe **only the network edge**; the dialog must
  claim **nothing** about messaging, because what a disconnect does to an existing thread is D9's
  still-open half. **Whether a non-accepted row gains a message-request affordance is open build
  design, not a correction** — it rides the request-object shape and is decided nowhere.
  **The order matters:** Login first, because it is public. Neither is a redesign; both are copy.
- **[D30](#7-open-decisions) is decided and the code has not caught up — ~~three changes are owed, none of them made~~ one is owed, and it is the server's**
  (2026-08-29). ✅ **(b) and (c) landed the same day** (`0911948`, `2c225ca`), in the order this item
  prescribed. The React client stores nothing at Register, `RegisterResponse` no longer declares
  `accessToken`, and a successful registration navigates to Login. **(a) is untouched and is now the
  whole of what remains:** `POST /api/auth/register` still answers `201` with an `accessToken` and a
  `Set-Cookie`, confirmed against the running API rather than read off the source. The consequence to
  carry until it lands: a browser that registers holds a Refresh cookie for a session nothing will ever
  use. The original text follows, because its ordering argument is why the client went first. Recording the gap rather than closing it in a documentation pass, because each is a
  behaviour change that deserves its own approved piece of work. **(a) Register must stop issuing
  tokens.** `registration.service.ts` still calls the token pair and the controller still sets the
  Refresh cookie, so `POST /api/auth/register` answers `201` with an `accessToken` and a
  `Set-Cookie` — a credential the approved flow now guarantees nobody uses. Removing it also
  changes the endpoint's documented contract in `Backend/README.md` and `requests/auth.http`.
  **(b) The React client must stop storing that token.** `useRegisterForm` calls `setAccessToken`
  on success, which after (a) would be storing `undefined`; `RegisterResponse` loses `accessToken`
  too. **(c) Register must navigate to Login**, which it cannot do yet — see the item below.
  **The order matters:** (b) before (a), or a deployed client briefly stores a token the server has
  stopped sending. Until all three land, the shipped behaviour is the pre-D30 one, and that is the
  honest reading of the code.
- ~~**The approved `Register → Login → Personal dashboard` flow has one screen in React out of three**~~
  ✅ **Closed 2026-08-29 (`0911948`).** Login is migrated, `react-router` is a dependency, and the flow
  is wired end to end. **The dashboard is the honest part to state:** approved screen #5 is *not*
  migrated — it is still only on the unmerged `feature/personal-dashboard` branch — so `/dashboard`
  renders a placeholder that says exactly that and builds none of the eight widgets. It borrows the
  profile stylesheet for the app shell so that nothing has to be unpicked when the real screen arrives:
  that file is deleted whole. **The shortcut this item rejected was never taken** — nothing navigates to
  `screens/login.html`. Original entry below.
  (2026-08-29). Register is migrated; **Login exists only as `screens/login.html`** and the
  **Personal dashboard only on the unmerged `feature/personal-dashboard` branch**, so it is not on
  `develop` at all. `react-router` is not a dependency yet. The tempting shortcut — navigating from
  the React app to the static `login.html` — is **rejected**: Vite does not serve `screens/` in a
  production build, so it would work in development only and be deleted the moment Login is
  migrated. The flow therefore stays unwired until Login is a React screen, and the Register
  success state remains the honest end of the road.
- **~~`register.css`~~ every migrated screen's stylesheet now exists twice, and the copies are allowed to differ** (2026-08-28, widened 2026-08-29 with the
  Login, profile and password-reset migrations). **It is now five files, not one:** `register.css`,
  `login.css`, `profile.css`, `my-profile.css` and `edit-profile.css` each have a `screens/` copy and a
  `src/features/…` copy, and `src/features/password-reset/password-reset.css` is a sixth file with no
  static twin — it holds only what the two password screens add on top of the auth sheet, so Login does
  not carry rules no Login element uses. **The rule this item set held**: the React copies were made by
  copying the file and rewriting only the mechanisms JavaScript replaces — the `.he-text`/`.en-text`
  duplication, the `.lang-radio`, the `:has()` reveals and the CSS-only submit gates — so every
  declaration that carries the approved visual is byte-identical. **Measured, not asserted:** a
  computed-style comparison of the static screen against its React counterpart, in both languages,
  reports zero differences on Login, Register, Forgot password and Reset password, and zero on the
  Hebrew profile screens. **The resolution is still deletion, not synchronisation.** Original entry
  follows. `screens/register.css` still serves the static prototype; `src/features/register/register.css`
  serves the React screen. The split was deliberate rather than careless: the prototype has **no
  JavaScript**, so it still needs the `he-field`/`en-field` twin mechanism and the CSS `:has()`
  submit gating that [D10](#7-open-decisions) removed from the React copy — editing one file to
  serve both would have broken a screen that [D12](#7-open-decisions) requires to keep opening by
  double-click. **About 1,180 of 1,272 lines are identical**, which is exactly what makes the drift
  dangerous: a visual fix applied to one copy will look applied to both. **The resolution is
  deletion, not synchronisation** — when the React Register is accepted, `screens/register.html`
  and `screens/register.css` are removed together, and this item closes with them. Until then,
  treat the React copy as the live one. The same pattern will repeat once per migrated screen, so
  **the standing rule from 2026-08-28 is that a brand-new screen is built directly in React** and
  never gets a static prototype it would immediately owe a second copy of.
- **Scope volume is the real enemy.** ~36 screens for a solo project. Survive it with *ruthless
  component reuse* (one generic list, one generic form) and by cutting from the back of the roadmap.
- **Privacy leak risk (highest technical risk).** The danger isn't the UI — it's a single endpoint that
  returns a full task object and leaks the delegate/private layer. Mitigation: all task data exits the
  server through one viewer-aware serializer; add explicit *"GC must NOT see X"* tests. This privacy/
  authorization suite is the one place automated tests genuinely earn their keep — most other testing
  can be manual.
- **Authorization is a separate concern from the serializer.** The serializer governs what a user can
  *see* (read side). It says nothing about what a user is allowed to *do* (write side): reschedule this
  task, invite to this project, delegate this work. That check ("can this user perform this action on
  this resource?") must also be centralized, or it ends up scattered and inconsistent across controllers.
  **Where it lives is now decided:** the `memberships` collection is the authorization table — one helper
  resolves `(user, project) → role | null` and every controller asks it the same question. See
  [`docs/database-design.html`](docs/database-design.html) → Collections → `memberships`. The two checks
  must stay distinct: a project member is *authorized* to read a task and still must not *see* its delegate.
- **DAG cycles.** Creating dependency edge X→Y must be rejected if Y is already an ancestor of X, or the
  cascade loops forever. **X and Y are STAGES, not tasks** — owner decision 2026-08-30, against the closed
  finding `אין דבר כזה בבניין עבודה ספציפית, כל עבודה בבניין היא שלב`. Cycle prevention shipped with the
  stage-dependency editor (`PATCH /api/projects/:projectId/stages/:stageId/dependencies`), **not** on a
  task screen; ~~the dependency picker (Stage 3)~~ named a task-level control that was never built.
  **The risk itself is undiminished** — the dependency domain is live and load-bearing; only the
  task-level *representation* is superseded.
- **Cascade complexity creep.** Start with naive delta-propagation (shift dependents by the same amount).
  *Not* critical-path slack analysis unless there's spare time.
- **The cascade × one-round negotiation is underspecified — design it on paper before coding Stage 4.**
  The graph traversal itself is textbook; what's undefined is the negotiation on top of it. The specific
  failure mode: when the cascade fans out to several parties who vote independently, some accept and some
  reject, leaving a **partially-applied cascade and an inconsistent schedule**. Before writing cascade
  code, draw the proposal state machine and decide the rules (per-task vs. bundled, what "one round"
  means, whether a counter re-triggers a cascade). ~~Options are parked in **D2**.~~
  **Annotated 2026-08-29: the negotiation rules are now closed, so the options are no longer parked.**
  [D2](#7-open-decisions) closed as policy against the 2026-08-24 / 2026-08-28 findings — per-task
  proposals, automatic full-chain movement with per-affected approval, 72-hour expiry that hands the
  decision back to the GC, partial approval at the GC's sole discretion, and counter re-cascade closed
  by entailment. **The advice above still stands and is still worth following**: drawing the state
  machine on paper before writing cascade code is good practice whether or not the rules are settled,
  and the specific failure mode it names — a partially-applied cascade across parties who answer
  independently — is exactly what the GC-discretion rule now resolves rather than prevents. What
  changed is only that the drawing is now a transcription of decided rules, not a decision.
- **Heroku gotchas.** Filesystem is ephemeral (wiped on restart → never store files on disk) and there's
  no free tier anymore (small monthly cost — check current pricing; syllabus mandates Heroku so budget for it).
- **Websocket temptation.** The app *feels* real-time, but real-time is not required. Resist Socket.io.
- **Admin suite is a second app.** Orthogonal to core value and not in the syllabus — build last, trim first.
- **Date / timezone handling.** The whole app is dates and "X days late" math — a classic bug source.
  Standardize early: store dates in UTC and use a date library rather than hand-rolling arithmetic.
- **RTL/LTR is a tax on every screen, and it fails silently.** A hard-coded `left` or `right` in CSS is a
  bug that looks perfect in one language and broken in the other, so it survives review easily. Mirroring
  mistakes are worse than missing translations — a back arrow pointing the wrong way reads as *broken*,
  not as *untranslated*. Second half of the problem is **text length**: English strings are usually longer
  than their Hebrew equivalents, so a layout tuned until it just fits in Hebrew will overflow in English
  (buttons, table headers, and the navbar are the usual casualties). Mitigations: logical properties only,
  no fixed widths on anything containing text, and the both-directions check is part of the definition of
  Done. Full rules in [§3.4](#34-bilingual--bidirectional-ui-hebrew-rtl-default--english-ltr).
- **A logical property is not a direction, and §3.4 rule 2 alone does not catch this.** Found on
  2026-08-25 on the auth screens: `.lang-switch` was positioned with `inset-inline-end`, obeying the
  never-`left`/`right` rule exactly, and still never moved — because a logical inset on a fixed-position
  element resolves against **that element's own** direction, and the pill sits *outside* the containers
  that carry `direction: rtl`. **This is a hazard on every screen that flips direction on a container
  instead of on `<html>`** (rule 1's recorded debt), for any element outside that container. `404.html`
  escapes it only because its pill happens to sit *inside* `.app`. The check that finds it is not a grep
  for `left`/`right` — it is reading computed `direction` back in both languages and failing any element
  whose value does not change. See [§10](#10-bug--issue-log). ✅ **Removed on the auth screens on
  2026-08-25 (`c6d53c5`)** — not by giving the pill a second direction of its own, but by moving
  `direction` onto `html`, which is what rule 1 prescribes. The pill now inherits it like every other
  element and the "outside the container" category stops existing on those three screens. **The hazard
  stands unchanged everywhere else** that still flips direction on a container.
- ~~**`register.html` still carries that exact bug and was not fixed.**~~ ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-27 (`15acaa3`) by copying `c6d53c5`, which is exactly what the last sentence of this item prescribed.** The original text is kept below because the reasoning in it is why the repair was cheap. Same `inset-inline-end`, same pill
  outside the direction-carrying containers, so its language toggle stays on one side in both languages.
  Left alone per the standing instruction not to modify another screen from this branch. `register.css`
  also has a `.lang-switch` media-query override that the repair will have to account for. **Do not repair
  it by copying `4e1cf33`.** That fix gave `.lang-switch` a `direction` of its own so its logical inset had
  something to resolve against; `c6d53c5` deleted those two declarations, along with the four on
  `.brand-panel__inner` and `.form-panel`, and set `direction` on `html` instead. Six per-container
  declarations became two at the root. Register should copy **`c6d53c5`**, which also fixes its unmirrored
  layout — a defect this entry never named, because the pill was the only symptom anyone had looked at.
  **The repair took the prescribed route and found one more defect on the way.** `direction` moved to `html`, the per-container declarations went, the unmirrored layout named above was fixed in the same commit, and the `.lang-switch` media-query override was accounted for. What nobody had named: scoped to `.auth-layout`, the English font swap never reached `body`, so `--font-sans` resolved against `:root` and **English copy had always computed Heebo instead of Inter**. See [§11](#11-decision-log) `2026-08-27`.
- ~~**The auth screens now place the language pill on the opposite corner from every other screen.**~~
  ✅ **Closed the same day (2026-08-25, `c6d53c5`) — the owner reversed the morning's decision.** The pill
  is back to **left in Hebrew, right in English**, matching `404.html`, `landing.html` and the navbar
  screens, so no project-wide divergence remains to settle before the React migration. What reversed it was
  the second-order effect this entry had flagged and not measured: once `c6d53c5` mirrored the *whole* page,
  the leading corner became the navy brand panel in **both** languages rather than one, and the cream pill
  sat on top of `.reg-mark--tl` and hid it. `inset-inline-start` became `inset-inline-end`, which puts the
  pill over the cream form panel in both languages — so the navy-panel contrast question this entry left
  open is now moot rather than answered. Both options were rendered and compared before the choice.
- **The current bilingual mechanism is known debt, not a pattern to copy.** Duplicating every string as
  `.he-text`/`.en-text` was the right call for JS-free static prototypes and is the wrong call for 36 React
  screens. It also leaves a real accessibility bug today: `<html lang="he">` is hard-coded, so English text
  is announced by screen readers with Hebrew pronunciation. Resolve it in the React migration (D10).
  **Fixed on the three auth screens on 2026-08-25 (`c6d53c5`), and only there:** `lang.ts` now sets
  `documentElement.lang` and `.dir` from the checked radio, on load and on every change. It is a JS repair
  to a CSS-only mechanism, so it is a **patch on the debt, not a discharge of it** — a visitor with
  JavaScript disabled still gets `lang="he"` while reading English, and the duplicate-markup pattern is
  untouched. Every other bilingual screen loads the same `lang.ts` and inherits the fix as soon as its
  branch picks up the commit.
  **Correction (2026-08-18): the original wording — "do not extend the pattern to new screens beyond the
  ones already built" — was unworkable and has been overtaken twice**, by subscriptions and again by
  my-profile. It cannot hold, because §3.4 rule 6 and the definition of Done require *every* screen in both
  languages, and there is no other way to do that with no JavaScript. The rule that actually applies: **do
  not invent a second bilingual mechanism** — every remaining static screen uses this one, and all of them
  are replaced at once by the React resource file.
- ~~**Free-text location cannot populate the region filter**~~ ✅ **Closed 2026-08-28 (`7c1e7be`, `feature/register-backend-fixes`)** — `register.html` now collects `city` as free text and `region` from the nine approved codes, copied verbatim from `edit-profile.html`. No screen writes location in a shape the schema cannot use any more. The original report is kept below because it is why the repair had to be a second field rather than a parser. (found while building my-profile, 2026-08-18).
  `screens/register.html` collects location as **one free-text field** ("תל אביב, מרכז"), but the schema
  stores `location { city, region, … }` and browse-contractors filters on a **`region` code** backed by
  `{ status: 1, specialties: 1, 'location.region': 1 }`. Free text can never populate that index, so the
  region filter would return nothing for anyone who registered but never opened their profile.
  **Resolved at the contract, 2026-08-28:** rather than parse or store the combined string, `POST /api/auth/register` takes `city` and a canonical `region` **code** as two fields and rejects display text — so the backend can never write an unusable region. My-profile
  now collects city as text and region from the fixed list, and **`register.html` was the last screen that
  did not** — which `7c1e7be` closed, on the screen, three hours after the contract did.
- ~~**`register.html` has the language toggle but never loads `lang.js`**~~ ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-27 (`15acaa3`)** — the screen now loads the same shared `lang.js` every other bilingual screen loads, and persistence across a reload was confirmed in headless Edge driving the real `עב` / `EN` labels rather than setting radio state directly. Originally found during the shared-script audit,
  2026-08-18). Every other bilingual screen — login, forgot-password, reset-password, subscriptions, and both
  profile screens — loads it, so the choice persists across page loads. On register it does not: switching to
  English and moving to the next screen silently returns to Hebrew. Belongs to `feature/register-screen`;
  it is a one-line `<script>` addition, not a redesign.
- ~~**`register.ts` is redundant but cannot be deleted yet.** `validation.ts` now covers everything it did
  (`refactor/shared-ui`, `ccbfdc7`).~~ ✅ **Consolidated 2026-08-28. The claim above was wrong, and acting on
  it as written would have broken the screen.** `validation.ts` did **not** cover everything `register.ts` did:
  it bound `.form-input` on `blur` only, while `register.ts` bound `.form-input`, **`.form-select` and
  `.checkbox-input`** on **`blur` and `change`**. Register is the only auth screen with selects (2) and a
  consent checkbox (1) — `login.html` has neither — and `change` is what those two control types actually
  commit on, since blur is unreliable for a control changed by click or by keyboard. Swapping the
  `<script src>` on the strength of the original sentence would have silently killed the red state on
  Register's trade select and its terms checkbox; `register.css` lines 861 and 870 are the rules that would
  have stopped matching. **The resolution was to widen the shared module rather than delete the specific
  one:** `validation.ts` now takes all three selectors and both events, `register.html` loads
  `validation.js`, and `register.ts` / `register.js` are deleted. **The touched-state implementation now
  exists in exactly one file.** Confirmed behaviour-preserving for the other four screens before the change:
  `login.html`, `forgot-password.html` and `reset-password.html` have no selects or checkboxes at all, and
  `edit-profile.html` has two selects but **`edit-profile.css` carries no `.form-select.touched` rule**, so
  the added class is inert there. Also confirmed that all 16 of Register's controls sit inside
  `.register-form`, so trading `register.ts`'s form scoping for the shared module's document-wide query
  reaches exactly the same elements. The `defer` attribute was dropped with the swap, matching the four
  sibling screens; at the end of `<body>` it was already a no-op.
- ~~**`login.css` mirrors its corner registration marks the wrong way**~~ ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-25 (`c6d53c5`),
  two lines exactly as predicted** — `border-right: 0` / `border-bottom: 0` became `border-inline-end: 0` /
  `border-block-end: 0` on `.reg-mark--tl`, and the mirror of that on `--br`. It was found independently a
  second time while moving `direction` to `html`, which is the point: the fix only became *visible* once the
  page actually mirrored, so for the five days this entry sat open the marks were pointing the wrong way in
  a language whose layout never flipped. Original entry below. (found while building the 404 screen,
  2026-08-20). `.reg-mark--tl` / `.reg-mark--br` position themselves with the logical `inset-inline-start` /
  `inset-inline-end`, so the marks correctly swap corners between Hebrew and English — but they *open* the
  corner with the **physical** `border-right: 0` / `border-left: 0`. The result is that in RTL each mark lands
  in the mirrored corner still pointing the way it pointed in LTR, so it brackets nothing. This is the exact
  failure mode [§3.4 rule 2](#34-bilingual--bidirectional-ui-hebrew-rtl-default--english-ltr) predicts: correct
  in one direction, wrong in the other, and easy to miss. `error.css` uses `border-inline-end` /
  `border-block-end` instead and is correct in both. Fix is two lines, on `feature/login-screen`.
- **`browse-contractors.html` has the language toggle but never loads `lang.js`** — the same defect §8 already
  records against `register.html`, found again on 2026-08-20 while auditing which screens load the shared script.
  Switching to English and navigating away silently returns to Hebrew. Belongs to `feature/browse-contractors-screen`;
  it is a one-line `<script src="lang.js"></script>` addition.
- **Static error screens cannot offer a "go back" control.** `history.back()` is JavaScript, and the prototypes
  are deliberately JS-free apart from the shared `lang.js`. The 404 therefore ships one absolute link to Home
  rather than a browser-history control. Worth revisiting in React, where a back affordance is one line — but
  note that on a 404 reached by a bad *typed* URL, "back" returns the user to wherever they came from, which is
  often not useful. Not a defect; a limit to remember when the error screens are ported.
- **The advanced-filters disclosure lost its native semantics (2026-08-22, `browse-contractors.html`).** The group was a `<details>`/`<summary>`, which announces as a disclosure widget with its expanded state, for free. Moving the fields into a panel beside the rail required an opener that is not an ancestor of what it opens, so it is now a visually-hidden checkbox driven by a `<label>`. It is focusable, labelled and keyboard-operable, and it carries `aria-controls` — but it **announces as a checkbox rather than as an expandable region, and `aria-expanded` cannot be maintained without JavaScript**. Keeping `<details>` was investigated and does not survive the layout: its body must be a descendant, so it would be clipped by the scrolling field list it has to escape. Accepted knowingly in exchange for the placement the owner asked for; it disappears in React, where the trigger becomes a real `<button aria-expanded>`. The same trade will face any other CSS-only disclosure that has to render outside its own scroll container.

- **The app navbar is copied per screen and has begun to drift** (found while building My Network, 2026-08-20). It was written as a self-contained block on `browse-contractors.html` so it would lift cleanly into a React `<Navbar/>`, but with no shared stylesheet each screen now owns a copy, and three defects are already visible. **(1) The *Network* link is `href="#"` on `browse-contractors.html`** — ✅ fixed in `ede1c52`; it points at `my-network.html`, which is dead until both branches reach `develop`. **(2) It overflowed the viewport by 1px between roughly 620px and 860px**, because the four links, the bell, the profile chip and the language pill stop fitting side by side well above the 620px breakpoint that moved the links to their own row. Fixed in `my-network.css` by wrapping the links from **800px** down instead; **every other screen carrying this navbar still has the 620px value** and therefore still has the overflow. **Re-measured on 2026-08-22 and still present** on `browse-contractors.html`: in English the document is 709px wide between roughly 630px and 700px of viewport, with `.app-nav__actions` the element crossing the edge, and Hebrew fits at the same widths. Confirmed byte-identical on the pre-review file, so it is inherited rather than newly introduced — left alone because the navbar is not what that review was about. **(3) `.nav-profile` and `.lang-switch` set their padding with the four-value physical shorthand** (`padding: 5px 11px 5px 7px`), so the tighter side does not follow the avatar when the layout mirrors — the [§3.4 rule 2](#34-bilingual--bidirectional-ui-hebrew-rtl-default--english-ltr) trap in a form that a `left`/`right` grep does not catch. Rewritten logically in `my-network.css` only. All three disappear when the navbar becomes one component; until then the two copies are knowingly 3 rules apart, recorded here rather than left to be discovered as a diff. **Added 2026-08-23:** `personal-dashboard.css` copies the **corrected 800px** version, so it does not inherit defect (2); but its navbar copy also points *Projects* at `my-projects.html` and *My Tasks* at `my-tasks.html`, where every other copy still has `href="#"` — a **fourth divergence**, taken deliberately so that no link on the Home screen is dead-by-`#` while its sibling widgets link to real filenames. It is on the list to collapse with the rest.
- **The Personal dashboard links to six filenames that do not exist yet** (2026-08-23). `personal-dashboard.html` reserves and links `my-tasks.html` (#15 My Tasks), `task-detail.html` (#16 Task detail), `my-projects.html` (#11 My projects), `create-task.html` (#17 Create / edit task or job), `create-project.html` (#12 Create / edit project) and `reschedule-proposal-review.html` (#18 Reschedule proposal review). This is the practice the 404 screen set — **reserve the approved screen's own name so the branch that builds it cannot pick a different one** — and it is the reason `personal-dashboard.html` itself was already waiting under the right name. **The cost is stated rather than hidden: every one of the six is a dead link until that screen is built**, which is a larger surface than any previous screen has carried. The alternative, `href="#"`, is worse: it is the dead control the project has rejected twice, and it leaves the next screen free to invent a different filename. `inbox.html`, `my-network.html` and `browse-contractors.html` were already reserved and are unaffected. **Updated 2026-08-23:** one of the six is now built — `my-projects.html` exists on `feature/my-projects` (`a791231`), so the dashboard's *Respond* control resolves as soon as both branches reach `develop`. **My projects reserved no new filename of its own**, which is the point of the practice working: everything it links (`project-dashboard.html`, `create-project.html`, `create-task.html`, `my-tasks.html`) was already reserved by the 404 screen or by the dashboard, and it deliberately added **no** link to approved screen #14 *Project members*, whose filename nobody has reserved yet — placing one would have invented both a filename and a permission the MD does not define.
- **The `OPEN_DECISIONS` array in `docs/database-design.html` has drifted behind §7** (found 2026-08-23 while adding D18). That array **stopped at `D11` at the time** — it runs to `D30` now, and the reconciliation note at the end of this item is the current state: `D13` (completed-work storage), `D14` (availability and heavy equipment) and `D17` (connection teardown) were opened in the MD and never added to it, even though all three name specific collections. `D18` **was** added, because golden rule 3 applies to the sitting that opens a decision — which makes the list look arbitrary until the older three are backfilled. Recorded rather than fixed in passing, because backfilling three decisions somebody else reasoned about is a documentation change that deserves its own review, not a side effect of building a screen. **Corrected 2026-08-23 (same day, after the audit): this finding was itself incomplete — it missed `D15` (phone visibility), which meets its own stated criterion.** `D15` names [[users]] and [[connections]], and the schema page was *already* citing it as an open decision in two places (the `users.phone` field note and the public-profile projection rule) while having no row for it — so the count was **four** missing, not three. ✅ **Resolved:** `D13`, `D14`, `D15` and `D17` were backfilled in the repair below; `D4` (repo layout) and `D12` (compiled-JS policy, closed) are intentionally excluded as not data-model decisions, and `D16` is excluded as an API/privacy-behaviour decision — the exclusion rule is now stated on the page itself so the next reader cannot mistake "out of scope" for "forgotten." The audit also found that the array had **never** contained any of them: nothing was ever removed, and the 2026-08-20 renumbering never touched it. **The drift resumed immediately, and this is the second time it is being recorded rather than repaired.** As of 2026-08-26 the array runs `D1–D24` plus `D27` and is missing **`D25`** (Terms and Privacy screens — arguably outside the array's stated scope, being a product-surface question that names no collection) and **`D26`** (delegation discovery — which *does* qualify, since its §7 entry turns on [[memberships]] carrying no record of who may delegate). `D27` was added by the sitting that opened it, per golden rule 3; `D26` is left for the same reason the four backfills above were originally deferred — reconciling a decision somebody else reasoned about is a documentation change deserving its own review, not a side effect of building a register screen. ✅ **Reconciled 2026-08-28 in a pass of its own** — the review both deferrals were waiting for, run as documentation only: **no product decision was changed, opened, closed or renumbered.** `D26` was added as an **open, in-scope** row; it names [[memberships]] and [[tasks]], and the condition it needs — *may this viewer delegate this task* — is exactly what the model cannot express. `D28` and `D29` were added **marked `closed`**, and `D10` and `D27`, which had closed in §7 while still rendering here as open, were marked the same way. **That the array keeps closed rows at all is the part worth stating:** a closed decision is what explains why a field has the shape it has, so removing it would delete the reasoning; `D7` had been carrying that convention alone since it closed. `D25`, `D16` and `D30` stay **excluded**, and `D30`'s rationale now sits on the page beside the other three — it is a **client navigation** question, and the one field it touches (`users.profileComplete`) exists already and is unchanged whichever way it closes. **One gap this pass recorded rather than repaired:** the [[companies]] and company-membership collections `D28` created are described in [§3.2](#32-data-model-entity-index--full-design-in-the-companion-html) and have **no entry on the schema page at all**, so both new rows point at [[users]] and say so in their own text.
- **Nothing may turn Overdue into a reminder.** Approved on 2026-08-23 with the Personal dashboard: an overdue task appearing on Home is **operational visibility only** and must not, by itself, create repeated reminders or repeated notification pressure. Today the model already respects this by accident rather than by rule — [[notifications]] rows are written at **write time** by the action that causes them, and there is no scheduler anywhere in the design, so no code path exists that could emit a daily "still overdue" row. The trap is that adding one would be easy and would look helpful: a `task_overdue` notification type, or a cron sweep over `{ endDate: { $lt: now }, publicStatus: { $ne: 'completed' } }`. This is also the §1 **"no progress nagging"** non-goal in a new costume, which is why it belongs here rather than only in the decision log.
- **A browse card cannot express two of the connection states it will meet.** ✅ **Fixed — `--incoming` on 2026-08-20 (`ede1c52`), `--blocked` on 2026-08-22 (`6a50657`).** `browse-contractors.html` rendered exactly three: no edge (*Connect*), request sent (*Pending*), accepted (*Connected*). It had nothing for **"this person sent *you* a request"** or **"you blocked them"**, both of which are real `connections` rows the directory will return. The first was the harmful one: the card showed *Connect*, inviting a request that the unique `pair` index must reject — the user experiences a dead button with no explanation. Both states now exist as `contractor-card--incoming` and `contractor-card--blocked`, and the rule behind them is stated once rather than per card: **Connect renders only where there is no relationship at all**, and every other state is reported as a status. Found while building My Network (2026-08-20) by checking that no contractor appears in a contradictory state on the two screens. **One caveat on the blocked half:** the sample set had no blocked contractor, so card 9 (Khaled Mansour) was converted into one to make the state visible on the screen rather than only reachable by editing the file — the same trade the My Network entry made when it shipped Blocked empty, in the opposite direction. Whether a blocked contractor should appear in browse results at all was a product question nobody had answered. **Answered by the owner on 2026-08-23, against the caveat: they must not appear.** Blocking removes a contractor from discovery, so Browse has **no blocked card, no blocked badge and no blocked state** — `--blocked` and the CSS only it used were removed in `a911626` and the screen is back to four card states. The consequence to carry into Stage 2 is that this is a **query rule, not a rendering rule**: the directory query has to exclude contractors with a `blocked` [[connections]] row where the viewer is the blocker, so a blocked contractor is never returned rather than returned and hidden. **And the model cannot currently express that clause — see [D19](#7-open-decisions), opened by this review.** [[connections]] carries `requester`, `recipient`, `pair`, `status`, `requestedAt`, `respondedAt` and **nothing that records who did the blocking**. `requester` means *who sent the connection request*, which is a different fact and may well be the other party: if A and B were connected and A then blocks B, the row flips to `blocked` with `requester` still pointing at whoever originally asked. So the schema can answer *"are these two blocked"* and cannot answer *"did A block B, or B block A"* — which is exactly the direction this rule needs, because it excludes contractors blocked **by the viewer** and says nothing about the reverse. Flagged rather than assumed away; the prototype is unaffected either way. Note the asymmetry it creates with **My Network**, which still shows Blocked as its own group and is the only place the state is visible or reversible; that is deliberate, and it is why both request states on a browse card link there.
- **Deleting a chat or conversation is not defined anywhere in the project.** Approved screen **#19** lists *mute-conversation* only; [[threads]] carries no `deletedFor`, `hiddenFor` or `archivedAt` field; and [D9](#7-open-decisions) asks only what happens to a thread on disconnect, block or mute — never what a user-initiated delete would do. Raised by the owner on 2026-08-20 as an existing capability while My Network was being built; the search above is what the repository actually contains, so **no delete control was placed on My Network** ~~and none should be added anywhere until this is decided~~ ***(the blanket is superseded 2026-08-29 — the user layer is decided below, so a control may now be placed against the decided rules rather than withheld.)*** ~~The questions it needs to answer are the usual ones and none is free: is a delete **per-participant** (a `deletedFor: [userId]` array, so the other side keeps the conversation) or **mutual**; does it remove the [[messages]] or only the thread from one inbox;~~ ***(both answered — per-participant, for the whole chat; and the two are separate operations rather than one with a parameter.)*** **The one question of the three that survives, and it is this item's remaining question: does a later message from the other party bring back a chat one side deleted?** Not decided, and not to be inferred. With it survive the **schema shapes this item names** — `deletedFor` on [[threads]], a per-message deleted state on [[messages]], and the kept marker the delete-for-everyone path checks — which are **owed shapes, not open decisions**. Note that per-participant deletion is the only variant that does not let one person destroy another person's record of an agreement — which matters here, because §1 says everything is logged — **and that argument is the one that won.**
  ✅ **Superseded 2026-08-29 — the blanket is replaced, not merely qualified. The user-deletion layer was decided by the owner; what remains open is narrower and is named below.**
  ~~none should be added anywhere until this is decided~~ no longer holds, and the item's own instinct was right: **the variant it argued for is the one that was chosen.**
  **(a) A whole chat deletes per-side only.** A user may delete an entire chat **from their own side**: it disappears from their list and history, **remains fully available to the other participant**, and is **never** a global deletion. That answers the per-participant-versus-mutual question.
  **(b) An individual message deletes for everyone.** The author may remove one of their own messages for all participants; the content goes, **its position stays as a neutral placeholder** — `הודעה זו נמחקה` / *"This message was deleted."* — and the rest of the conversation is unchanged. **One exception, (e) below.**
  **(c) So the messages-versus-thread question is answered by there being two different operations**, not one with a parameter: chat delete hides a thread from one inbox; message delete removes content for all.
  **(d) Reported content survives deletion.** If a message was **reported before it was deleted**, deletion does **not** remove the internal moderation copy — it is retained under the moderation rules already defined. **User-visible deletion and internal moderation retention are separate concepts** and must stay separate.
  **(e) A GC keep overrides the author's delete-for-everyone.** Where the GC has **explicitly decided a message must be kept**, the author **cannot** delete it for everyone: **the original message itself is preserved** — not a separate retained copy — and it is **never** replaced by the deleted-message placeholder. This is distinct from (d): a *reported* message may still leave the user-visible conversation while the moderation copy survives; a *kept* message stays where it is. **No new retention rule and no new permission was created** — this defines the intersection of two rules that were already closed.
  **(f) Removal-scoped deletion is the GC's per-case choice.** When a professional is removed from a project, `לפעמים מוחקים את ההודעות ולפעמים לא` — the GC, who owns the conversation, decides **case by case** whether the removed party's messages are kept or deleted. **Never automatic.** The lawsuit example is exactly why *keep* has to exist.
  **The GC's broader deletion authority is preserved unchanged** — Project-Room moderation removal with its neutral tombstone, and the removal-time keep-or-delete choice. Nothing above narrows it.
  **Standing vocabulary rule, so this does not drift again:** every deletion discussion in this project distinguishes **(a) own-view chat delete · (b) for-everyone message delete · (c) GC-authorized deletion · (d) internal moderation retention**. Four concepts, four names — the same don't-reuse-a-nearby-word discipline [D17](#7-open-decisions), [D18](#7-open-decisions) and [D24](#7-open-decisions) already apply to statuses. In particular, **D17's connection teardown is not chat deletion.**
  **What is still open, explicitly:** whether a later message from the other party **brings back a chat one side deleted** — *not decided, do not infer it*; the **schema shapes** — per-viewer hidden state on [[threads]], per-message deleted state on [[messages]], the placeholder rendering path, and the **kept marker** the delete-for-everyone path checks — all **owed, none chosen**; and whether the **moderation tombstone shares the deleted-message wording**. `docs/database-design.html` changes in the same sitting as whichever shape is chosen.
- **"Owner" has two candidate definitions in the schema and they can disagree** (found 2026-08-23 while building My projects). The screen's primary axis needs one, and the model offers two: `projects.owner`, described as *"the creator, and the general contractor of record"*, and a [[memberships]] document with `role: 'gc'`. They are **deliberately duplicated** — the schema says so — but nothing anywhere forbids a *second* `gc` membership on a project the viewer does not own, and no rule says one exists. **The screen derives Owner from `projects.owner`**, because that is the field the design names as the contractor of record and the one the `{ owner: 1, status: 1 }` index was built to serve for exactly this screen. The consequence to know: a hypothetical non-owner `gc` renders as **Member** here while holding general-contractor write rights, so the badge would understate their authority. Cheap to resolve when Stage 3 writes the invite flow — either state that `gc` is unique per project, or derive the badge from the membership role instead. Flagged rather than assumed away; the prototype is correct under either answer for every card it actually shows.
- **Nothing defines what a project invitation may disclose before it is accepted** (found 2026-08-23 while building My projects). An invited [[memberships]] row fails `status: 'accepted'`, so the one authorisation query the backend is built on grants the invitee **no read on the project** — yet an invitation nobody can evaluate is useless, and the card has to say what the work is. The prototype shows **project name, city, start and target-end dates, who invited, and the offered role**, and deliberately withholds `stats.memberCount`, any task, and any pending-action count. That split is a judgement call with nothing behind it: [§3.3](#33-delegation-visibility-model-depth--1) governs delegation visibility and says nothing about invitations, and the viewer-aware serializer that would enforce it is Stage 5. Related to [D16](#7-open-decisions), which is the same family of question — what a viewer who is not permitted to see something is told. Decide with the serializer, not before.
- **`my-projects.html` carries the fifth copy of the app navbar, and it adds a state none of the others has.** Measured 2026-08-23 across all branches: `browse-contractors.css`, `my-network.css`, `profile.css`, `personal-dashboard.css` and now `my-projects.css`. It copies the **corrected** wrap (links take their own row from **800px** down) so it does not inherit defect (2) below, and it uses the logical chip padding so it does not inherit defect (3). What is new is that it is the **first navbar that points at its own page**, so its *Projects* link carries `is-active` and `aria-current="page"` — a divergence taken deliberately, because a nav item that links to the page you are on and does not say so is worse than the duplication. It collapses with the rest when the navbar becomes one component. **One correction to the note below while measuring:** its claim that *"every other screen carrying this navbar still has the 620px value"* is no longer true — only `browse-contractors.css` does; `profile.css` wraps at 780px and the other three at 800px, and the measured failure band was roughly 630–700px, which 780 and 800 both clear. `my-projects.html` was swept at 800 / 780 / 700 / 660 / 620px in **both** directions with no horizontal overflow.
- **Approved screen #11 says "filter/sort" and only the filter ships.** The role filter is four hidden radios and works; **sort is not built and was not faked.** A CSS-only sort means writing an `order` value per card by hand, which is not a sort — it is a second hand-maintained ordering that representative static data would silently invalidate the moment a card is added, and the screen would then claim a capability it does not have. Same family as the *static error screens cannot offer a "go back" control* note above: a real limit of the no-JavaScript prototype, recorded rather than papered over. It is one line in React, where the list is data.
- **Three Hebrew role labels on My projects are new copy and are not approved** (2026-08-23). The badges read **`בעלות` / `חברות` / `הזמנה`** (Owner / Member / Invited). They are **noun forms chosen because they stay gender-neutral**, following exactly the reasoning [D14](#7-open-decisions) used for the shortened availability labels — an adjective or a participle here would inflect for the viewer's gender, which the project has already ruled out once. That makes them defensible, not approved. Confirm or replace them before this ships. The per-project role codes beside them (`קבלן ראשי` / `קבלן משנה` / `ספק` / `צפייה בלבד`) are **not** new — they reuse the wording already on the Personal dashboard and the profile screens.
- **`docs/database-design.html` presents the biggest sub-question of D5 as answered, while §7 still records D5 as open** (found 2026-08-23 while building My Tasks). §7 lists D5 as *Open* and states the sub-question in both directions — *"do standalone jobs and project tasks share one polymorphic model (nullable project, no dependencies) or split into two models?"* The schema page answers it: its `D5` row says *"This design says **yes** — one [[tasks]] collection with a nullable `project`"*, the `tasks` collection is fully modelled that way, its `project` field note reads *"The one field that makes the polymorphic model work (decision D5)"*, and a `why` entry is titled *"One collection for tasks and standalone jobs (this is the answer to D5)"*. **This is the same class of drift the 2026-08-23 audit corrected for GridFS and for the 404** — a worked design being read back as an approved decision — with one difference worth stating: here the schema page argues its case in the open and the reasoning is good (splitting would duplicate most of the fields and force this very screen to query two collections and sort in memory). **No owner approval for it exists anywhere in this document.** Recorded rather than corrected in passing, because closing D5 or rewording the page is a decision, not a side effect of building a screen. **`my-tasks.html` is unaffected either way** — it renders the source tag from *"is there a project"*, which is one field under one shape and one list membership under the other, and it says nothing about storage.
- **Two Personal-dashboard tasks are completed and under an open date negotiation at the same time** (found 2026-08-23 while building My Tasks, and **not repaired**). `personal-dashboard.html` shows *Pour ground-floor slab* @ Rothschild 42 as **completed 18 August** in *Waiting for your rating*, and simultaneously as the subject of a **date-change request whose response window closes 25 August** in *Pending actions*. The same pattern holds for *Roof waterproofing* @ Rothschild 42: **completed 12 August** in the rating widget, and a proposal the viewer sent on **21 August** that closes 26 August in *Waiting on others*. Renegotiating the dates of finished work is not a state the product has — and the rating gate is specifically *shared **completed** work*, so the completion is the half that cannot be soft. This is the same family as the Herzl 8 repair in [§10](#10-bug--issue-log): mock data that no single widget can see is wrong. **Left alone deliberately** — it lives on `feature/personal-dashboard`, and the instruction for this screen was not to modify unrelated screens. **My Tasks does not inherit it:** it puts the pending-proposal marker on *Plaster finish, floor 2*, an open task at the same project with the same 25 August window, so the viewer still has exactly **one** proposal awaiting an answer and no completed row is under negotiation. Repair the dashboard on its own branch.
- **Approved screen #15 says "filter/sort" and none of the four controls is wired** (2026-08-23). Project, Status, Due date and Type are native `<select>` elements that change value and filter nothing. Two of the four *could* have been made live with the hidden-radio pattern the dashboard's solo-job filter uses — status and type are class predicates — but **project** is a list the markup would have to enumerate by hand and **due-date sort** is the hand-written `order` that My projects already refused to fake. A bar where two controls work and two do not is harder to read than one that is uniformly not yet wired, so all four are inert and say so here. This follows the **Browse precedent** (a full filter rail, real filtering wired in React at Stage 2) rather than the My-projects one (sort omitted entirely) — the difference is that Browse's controls are *unimplemented*, whereas a fake sort would have been *wrong*. The layout is the part that has to survive: each control is a labelled `<select>` that becomes a controlled component with no change to the grid.
- **No create control was placed on My Tasks, and its empty state carries no CTA** (2026-08-23). Approved screen **#17** is *Create / edit task or job* and `create-task.html` is already reserved and linked from the Personal dashboard, so a *New job* control here would have had somewhere to point — but #15's approved wording does not include one, and the owner's instruction for this screen was not to introduce unapproved CTAs. The consequence to weigh: a viewer whose list is empty is told what would appear here and given **no way to start anything from this screen**. Flagged rather than decided; adding the control later is one anchor.
- **Seven labels on My Tasks are new copy and are not approved** (2026-08-23). Two group titles — `עבודה פתוחה` / *Open work* and `הושלמו` / *Completed*; the two counterparty strings — `נוצרה על ידכם` / *Created by you* and the `מאת` / *From* prefix; the two row controls — `התחלה` / *Start* and `סיום` / *Complete*; and the proposal marker sentence, `הצעה לתאריך חדש ממתינה לתשובתכם · עד 25 באוגוסט` / *A new date is waiting for your answer · by 25 August*. All seven are **noun or past-participle forms that agree with the task, never with the viewer**, so they stay gender-neutral for the same reason [D14](#7-open-decisions) and the My-projects role labels do — defensible, not approved. Confirm or replace them before this ships. **Start and Complete are the pair worth looking at hardest**, because they are the two words the whole binary tracking model is named after in §1 and this is the first screen to render them as controls; every later screen that offers the same actions should use whatever wording is settled here. What is **not** new: the third group title `באיחור` / *Overdue*, the three status labels `טרם התחילה` / `בביצוע` / `הושלמה`, the source tag `עבודה עצמאית` / *Solo job*, `פתיחה` / *Open*, and the lateness and due-date phrasings, all lifted from `personal-dashboard.html`. The completion line `הושלמה 14 באוגוסט · היעד היה 15 באוגוסט` extends the dashboard's `הושלמה 18 באוגוסט` with a second clause, which is a small addition rather than a new form. Group ledes are per-screen prose and are not counted here.
- **`my-tasks.html` carries the sixth copy of the app navbar** (2026-08-23). Measured across all branches: `browse-contractors.css`, `my-network.css`, `profile.css`, `personal-dashboard.css`, `my-projects.css` and now `my-tasks.css`. It copies the **corrected** wrap (links take their own row from **800px** down) and the logical chip padding, so it inherits neither defect (2) nor defect (3) below, and it is the **second** navbar to point at its own page — its *My Tasks* link carries `is-active` and `aria-current="page"`, exactly as My projects does for *Projects*. It adds **no new divergence**. It collapses with the rest when the navbar becomes one component.
- **Create / edit project makes two dates required that the schema does not** (2026-08-23). The build instruction for screen #12 says *Start date — required* and *Target end date — required*, and the screen implements that: both carry `required`, a visible **חובה / Required** marker and a validation message. `docs/database-design.html` types them `startDate: Date` and `targetEndDate: Date` with **no `required: true`** — only `name` and `owner` are required on [[projects]], and the sample document is the only place both dates happen to be filled. So the UI and the schema disagree about whether a project can exist without a date window, and **the disagreement is load-bearing for [D22](#7-open-decisions)**: a project with no start date has no first working day, which is where working-day arithmetic begins. Not resolved here — the instruction was explicit and the screen follows it — but one of the two has to move before Stage 3 writes the model, and the cheaper direction is almost certainly to add `required: true` to both fields rather than to weaken the screen.
- **`projects.status` is collected on no screen, and Create / edit project is where that became visible** (2026-08-23). The enum is `planning | active | completed | archived`, [[projects]] gives it `default: 'planning'`, an index `{ status: 1, targetEndDate: 1 }` is built on it, and **My projects renders it on every card**. Nothing anywhere sets it. A project created through this screen would take the default and stay in `planning` forever, so the Active and Completed badges My projects shows have no write path behind them. **No status control was added here**, because approved screen #12's field list does not contain one and the instruction was not to widen it — but the gap is now stated rather than waiting to be discovered when the first project refuses to leave planning. Related to [D24](#7-open-decisions), which is separately asking whether `archived` is allowed to mean two things.
  **Updated 2026-08-29 — the lifecycle itself is now closed as given (findings 2026-08-28), which changes what this gap is.** It is no longer *"nobody has said what the statuses mean"*; it is *"the meanings are decided and no write path implements them."* **A project becomes `started` when the GC has set a date AND the first task actually started on that date.** It becomes `completed` when **all works are finished to quality and every task is closed first** — closing tasks is a precondition, not a consequence, and small tasks left after the target date are finished to quality rather than waived. **Statuses are computed automatically from the working schedule** — which is consistent with the never-manages principle in [§1](#1-product-overview), because an automatic status is a **record**, not a decision taken on the contractor's behalf. **A frozen / paused state exists and the enum does not carry it** — stoppages are ordinary (rubbish, dirt, defective work, a pipe or cable crossing the site), so `planning | active | completed | archived` is short by at least one value. **Cancellation is pre-start only**, per [D24](#7-open-decisions). **The GC notifies the professionals** who were to take part. **And the date model is wider than the two fields the screen collects:** a project carries a **start date, a target end date, and an overrun allowance `x` set by the יזם** — `x` **may not be exceeded and is never edited**; when the target moves, the **actual overrun is recorded beside it** and **the original target is always kept**, so the GC can see he has not crossed the allowance. Stage 3 must build against this rather than against the bare enum.
- **Create / edit project carries the first control on any screen that is not a product control** (2026-08-23). The *Prototype state* switch picks between the Create form and the Edit form. It is honest — it genuinely swaps two real states, it is labelled `מצב אב־טיפוס` / *Prototype state*, and its own note says React turns these into two routes — but it is furniture that exists because a JavaScript-free file cannot change an input `value`, and it is the first time this project has put such a thing on a screen. The alternative considered and rejected was **a second `edit-project.html`**: it produces cleaner markup and no switch, at the cost of inventing a filename nobody reserved and leaving My projects' *Edit* control — which points at `create-project.html` — landing in the Create state. Worth re-deciding **only if the owner would rather review two files than one screen with a visible switch**; either way it disappears at the React migration, where the switch is deleted and the two forms become one component behind two routes.
- **"Reset to default" returns the panel to the inherited state and cannot un-tick the day boxes** (2026-08-23). The working calendar is two blocks swapped by one checkbox, and the day toggles in the customised block keep whatever the user last set. So *Reset to default* is truthful about what the screen then **shows** — the default week, in words and in chips — and a user who presses *Customize for this project* again will find their previous selection still there rather than the default. In a real implementation reset discards the override and re-reads the default; in CSS with no JavaScript there is no way to clear a checkbox from a different element. Recorded rather than faked, in the same family as the *static error screens cannot offer a "go back" control* and *sort was not built on My projects* notes: a real limit of the no-JavaScript prototype. One line in React.
- **The approved `--error` red now dresses a destructive control, not only a validation state** (2026-08-23). The exception was approved on 2026-06-07 for *validation states* on the login screen. `edit-profile.css` already stretched it once — `.work-item__remove:hover` turns red — and Create / edit project stretches it further: `.btn--danger` is `--error` text on `--surface` at rest and a filled `--error` on hover, the delete panel takes an `--error` inline-start edge, and the confirmation dialog takes an `--error` top edge. **No new hue was introduced** — every colour in the file is palette, the `#4A5D6E` gradient mid-stop, or `#A63232` — but the *scope* of an approved exception was widened without being asked, which is exactly the kind of quiet drift this document exists to catch. Flagged for confirmation. The fallback if it is refused is workable but weaker: the delete area would carry its meaning by placement, wording, an icon and separation alone, which the screen already does as well.
- **Ten strings on Create / edit project are new copy and are not approved** (2026-08-23). The load-bearing ones are the **working-calendar vocabulary** — `לוח ימי עבודה` / *Working calendar*, `משתמש בלוח ברירת המחדל שלכם` / *Using your default working calendar*, `לוח מותאם לפרויקט הזה` / *Customized for this project*, `התאמה לפרויקט הזה` / *Customize for this project* and `חזרה לברירת המחדל` / *Reset to default* — which are the first words this project has ever used for the concept and which every later calendar surface will have to match; the **required marker** `חובה` / *Required*; the two primary actions `יצירת הפרויקט` / *Create project* and `מחיקת הפרויקט` / *Delete project*; and the dialog title `למחוק את הפרויקט הזה?` / *Delete this project?*. All are noun or infinitive forms that agree with the object rather than with the viewer, so they stay gender-neutral for the same reason [D14](#7-open-decisions) and the My-projects role labels do — defensible, not approved. The ordinary field labels (`שם הפרויקט`, `תיאור`, `כתובת`, `תאריך התחלה`, `תאריך יעד לסיום`), the section titles (`פרטי הפרויקט`, `לוח זמנים`, `תמונות הפרויקט`) and the three validation messages are new too, but they are the plain names of the fields [[projects]] already defines. **Not new:** `עיר` / *City* and `אזור` / *Region* come from the profile screens, `שמירת שינויים` / *Save changes* and `ביטול` / *Cancel* from edit profile, and `פרויקט חדש` / *New project* from My projects.
- **`create-project.html` carries the seventh copy of the app navbar, and it is the first that points at nothing** (2026-08-23). Measured across all branches: `browse-contractors.css`, `my-network.css`, `profile.css`, `personal-dashboard.css`, `my-projects.css`, `my-tasks.css` and now `create-project.css`. It copies the **corrected 800px wrap** and the logical chip padding, so it inherits neither defect (2) nor defect (3) recorded above. What is new is that **no navbar item points at this screen**, so unlike My projects and My Tasks it carries no `is-active` and no `aria-current` at all — marking *Projects* as the current page would have been false, since that link goes to `my-projects.html`. It adds no other divergence and collapses with the rest when the navbar becomes one component.
- **The English half of a duplicated bilingual field has no `<label for>`, only an `aria-label`** (2026-08-23, inherited rather than introduced). Where a field carries a language-specific placeholder or value it is duplicated as `.he-field` / `.en-field`; the Hebrew copy owns the `id` the visible `<label>` points at, and the English copy carries `aria-label="Project name"` instead. So in English the visible label is **not programmatically associated** with the visible input, and clicking the label focuses a hidden field. This is exactly the pattern `edit-profile.html` established for city and bio, and it is one more cost of the duplicate-markup mechanism §3.4 already records as debt — it disappears with the React resource file, where one string exists per render and the label points at the only field there is. Not fixed here, because fixing it on one screen would make two screens disagree about the pattern.
- **Three controls on Create / edit project exist and do nothing, and one of them is destructive** (2026-08-23). *Add photos* / *Add photo*, the three per-photo remove buttons, and the *Delete project* button inside the confirmation dialog are all `<button type="button">` with no behaviour: a static prototype cannot open a file picker that leads anywhere, and what deletion does is [D24](#7-open-decisions). This is the same trade the Personal dashboard's *Dismiss permanently* button already makes, and it is **not** the *dead control* pattern the project has rejected three times — those were controls that could never succeed even when built, whereas these are unbuilt controls in their correct places. The one to watch is the delete confirmation: it is the only unbuilt control on any screen whose real behaviour is still undecided, which is why its dialog says so in words instead of implying an outcome.
- **Nothing on Create / edit project validates that the target end date follows the start date** (2026-08-23). Both fields are required and both are validated for emptiness; the relationship between them is not checked, because CSS cannot compare two input values and the prototype adds no JavaScript beyond the two tracked shared scripts. It is worth naming rather than leaving implicit, because it is the **first cross-field rule the product needs** and the date arithmetic in [§8](#8-known-issues--risks) above says this whole app is dates — a project whose target end precedes its start would make every working-day calculation in [D22](#7-open-decisions) meaningless. One JOI line on the server and one form-level check in React.
- **The Subscriptions screen is an authenticated account screen, and the Landing page links to it as a public one** (2026-08-24). This is the contradiction the Landing build was told to report rather than resolve, and it is a real one. Approved screen #1 is the *public face of the site*, and the owner's requirement is that a visitor can inspect plans **before** creating an account. `subscriptions.html` cannot serve that today: it carries the full `.app-nav` with a **notifications bell** and a **profile chip naming a specific user**, a *"Usage this week"* strip reading `You have created 1 of 3 visual simulations this week`, a **`Your plan`** flag on the Free card, and a **Billing history** link. Every one of those is meaningless or wrong for someone with no session. **The link ships anyway**, in both the navbar and the footer, per the standing rule that a gap in the model is not a reason to drop a control — and the Landing page states **no price, plan name or quota of its own**, so nothing has to be retracted when this is fixed. **The work this owes, stated concretely so it is actionable rather than merely noted:** `feature/subscriptions-screen` needs a signed-out variant that drops the app navbar (or swaps it for the public `.site-nav` this screen introduced), drops the usage strip, drops the `Your plan` flag, drops Billing history, and turns each card's action into *Create account* rather than a plan switch. Whether that is one screen branching on session or two files is open, and it is the same question [D10](#7-open-decisions) will face when the navbar becomes one component. Note the ordering trap: the public variant must not be built by *deleting* from the authenticated one, because the authenticated screen is the one the owner has already reviewed.
- **Body copy renders in the wrong language's font on every screen using the `.app` shell** (found 2026-08-24 while building the Landing page, and **it pre-dates that screen**). `body` sets `font-family: var(--font-sans)`, and the English swap redefines `--font-sans` on `#lang-en:checked ~ .app` — but `body` is the **ancestor** of `.app`, so it resolves the variable against `:root` and computes **Heebo** before `.app` can override anything. Everything that merely *inherits* its font therefore stays in the Hebrew sans while English is showing; only elements that **re-declare** `font-family: var(--font-sans)` themselves (`.btn`, `.lang-switch__btn`, the mono labels) pick up Inter. **Measured, not inferred:** `my-projects.html` switched to English renders `.projects__sub` in **Heebo** with `.app` correctly at `direction: ltr`. So the English screens have been reading in a Hebrew face — which is legible, because Heebo has Latin glyphs, and is exactly why nobody caught it. **The fix is one line — `font-family: var(--font-sans)` on `.app` itself**, which resolves the variable at the element that overrides it. Applied on `landing.css` only; `browse-contractors.css`, `my-network.css`, `profile.css`, `personal-dashboard.css`, `my-projects.css`, `my-tasks.css`, `create-project.css` and `subscriptions.css` all still need it on their own branches, and `login.css` / `register.css` / `error.css` need checking for the same shape. This is the [§3.4](#34-bilingual--bidirectional-ui-hebrew-rtl-default--english-ltr) *"fails silently in one direction"* tax in a form that a `left`/`right` grep cannot catch at all, and it disappears under React where one font stack is chosen per render. **✅ Confirmed gone on the four migrated screens (2026-08-29, `0911948`, `2c225ca`) — and the prediction needed one correction.** It does not disappear because React "chooses a stack per render"; it disappears because the migration moved `direction` **and** the font variables from `.app` to `<html>`, which is what [§3.4](#34-bilingual--bidirectional-ui-hebrew-rtl-default--english-ltr) rule 1 prescribes and which the `.app` copy could never satisfy while `body` was its ancestor. `.app` also lost its own `direction: rtl`, which would otherwise have out-specified the root and pinned the English screens to RTL. **Measured:** English My profile and Edit profile now compute **Inter** in `body`, where the static screens still compute Heebo. **A width consequence worth knowing before it is read as a regression:** `.profile__head-text` is `max-width: 92ch`, and `ch` resolves against the computed font — so the same element measures **818px** in the static English screen and **881px** in React. Nothing about the layout changed; the font under it did. **The eight static screens named above still need the one-line fix on their own branches.**
- **The Landing page reserves `help.html` and `contact.html` for two screens outside the approved 36** (2026-08-24). Both were placed by owner instruction — the footer is *Plans · Help & support · Contact*, and Contact is specified as a dedicated screen carrying a contact form. This follows the reservation practice the 404 screen set and the Personal dashboard extended: **name the screen now so the branch that builds it cannot pick a different filename.** The cost is the usual one and is stated rather than hidden — **both are dead links until those screens are built**, joining the five still outstanding from the Personal dashboard's original six.
