What is different here is that these two are **not on the lecturer-approved list at all**, so they widen scope rather than filling it in; that is an owner decision, recorded so it is visible when the 36-screen count is next reconciled. Neither has any specification beyond its name.
- **The Landing page is the only screen whose visual is a placeholder for another screen's output** (2026-08-24). The dependency chart stands in for a real screenshot of approved screen **#13**, which is not built. The risk is not that the placeholder is dishonest — it says what it is, in both languages, in its own caption — but that **it may never be swapped**, and a landing page still showing a hand-drawn approximation after the dashboard exists is worse than one that never claimed anything. Tie the swap to #13's own branch rather than leaving it to be noticed: when `project-dashboard.html` renders a real timeline, `landing.html` takes a screenshot of it and the `.depviz` block plus its caption are deleted together. Note also that a real screenshot is a **raster image with text in one language**, so the swap re-opens a question this chart answered for free — a screenshot cannot mirror or re-language itself, and will need either two images or an accessible text equivalent beside it.
- **Nine strings on the Landing page are new copy and are not approved** (2026-08-24). The load-bearing one is the **hero headline** — `משימות, תאריכים ותלויות — מתואמים במקום אחד, לא בטלפון.` / *Tasks, dates and dependencies — coordinated in one place, not over the phone.* — which is the first sentence this project has ever written to describe itself to a stranger, and which every later public surface will be measured against. Then the hero lede; the three section titles `מה שונה כאן` / *What is different here*, `איך זה עובד` / *How it works* and `מוכנים להתחיל?` / *Ready to start?*; the chart title `כך שינוי תאריך אחד עובר בשרשרת` / *How one date change moves through the chain*; and the footer line `FieldSync — אב־טיפוס אקדמי בפיתוח.` / *FieldSync — an academic prototype in development.* All are noun or infinitive forms that agree with the object rather than with the viewer, so they stay gender-neutral for the same reason [D14](#7-open-decisions) and the My-projects role labels do — defensible, not approved. Confirm or replace them before this ships. **What is deliberately not new:** the three value blocks restate `login.html`'s brand-panel strings almost verbatim, `יצירת חשבון` / *Create account* comes from `register.html` and `כניסה` / *Sign in* from `login.html`, and the five How-It-Works step labels are the owner's own wording from the build instruction. The four **example task names** in the chart (`קידוח כלונסאות`, `יציקת יסודות`, `שלד קומה 1`, `הכנת תשתיות חשמל`) are mock data, not product copy, and the caption says so.
- **The public confidential-delegation block was removed from `landing.html`** (2026-08-25, `3a9fd52` on `feature/landing-page`). The Landing page carried a titled value block — `האצלה חסויה` / *Confidential delegation* — whose body stated the mechanic in full: *"A contractor can pass work to a collaborator without the party above them seeing who is actually doing it."* It was accurate and it restated the approved [§3.3](#33-delegation-visibility-model-depth--1) model, which is exactly why it leaked: the block itself was the disclosure, on the public face of the site. It is gone, and **nothing was written in its place** — no softer wording, no indirect hint, no second explanation — per owner instruction, because any faithful description of the mechanism leaks the same fact. The section now carries **two** value blocks (equal users, trust signals) and `.values__grid` drops from three columns to two so no slot sits empty; verified in Edge headless at 1440px and 390px in both languages, no overflow and no horizontal scroll. **What is not fixed is the reason the block existed:** a contractor entitled to delegate now has no surface anywhere that tells them the capability exists. That is [D26](#7-open-decisions), still open, and **no replacement Landing value proposition may be invented before it closes**.
- **Login typography was realigned to the corrected Register scale, and two sibling-screen styles were left behind** (2026-08-25, `e33d935`). The earlier note-only fix settled one element and made the rest measurable: **fourteen** styles on `login.css` were smaller than their `register.css` counterparts, the brand panel worst of all — `.brand-sub` **14.5px** against Register's **19px**, `.brand-feature` **13.5px** against **18px**, `.secure-note` **10.5px** against **13px** and the beta badge at **9.5px** against **12px**. All fourteen now match their counterpart, and the containers grew with them exactly as `register.css` records for its own pass (`--panel-max` 440→500px, brand column 390→460px, inputs and buttons 48→54px), so the screen is not simply inflated type in an unchanged box. **The regression the pass caused, found by measurement rather than by eye:** the widened text column reached the two decorative crosshairs, which are positioned at a *percentage* of the panel — they overlapped the footer note in English at 1280px and the eyebrow in Hebrew at 1024px, where the pre-change baseline had zero overlaps. Both moved to the panel's padding band; re-measured across 11 width/language configurations, zero overlaps, nearest gap 14px. **Left behind deliberately, and they are a rule violation as they stand:** `.back-link` at **11px** and `.form-hint` at **12px** are both under the [§3.5](#35-shared-ui-rules-apply-to-every-screen) rule 6 floor, and neither appears on the Login screen — they belong to `forgot-password.html` and `reset-password.html`, which share the stylesheet and were out of scope for a Login pass. They need their own pass with those two screens open in a browser.
- **Demo seed data is a deliverable risk.** The Live Demo is graded, and a multi-party coordination
  platform is useless to demo from one empty account. You need a seed script (several users, a project
  with dependencies, a pending reschedule, a delegation). Build it incrementally as features land — not
  the night before.
- **Compiled JavaScript: resolved by policy (D12), but the branches still have to catch up.** The rule is
  now *track the TypeScript source, the compiled `.js`, and the build config together*, so a fresh clone
  opens every static screen by double-clicking. `develop` carries the sources, `tsconfig.json`,
  `package.json` and the built artifacts; `screens/*.js` was removed from `.gitignore` wherever it appeared.
  **The remaining trap is mechanical:** an ignore rule has no effect on an already-tracked path, so any
  future reversal of this policy needs `git rm --cached` and not just a `.gitignore` edit. Second trap: the
  build must be run from the tracked `tsconfig.json`, never ad hoc — `lang.js` and `validation.js` were
  originally compiled without it and therefore did not match what `npm run build` produces.
- **Eight Hebrew/English pairs on `register.html` say different things, and they are left that way on
  purpose** (found 2026-08-26 during the QA pass that shipped the phone fields). Each is either
  owner-approved terminology or a *product* choice rather than a translation error, so correcting them in
  passing would have set terminology as a side effect of editing a screen. **The two brand-panel bullets:**
  `תזמון מדורג` means *tiered scheduling*, not *cascade*, and `האצלת סמכות חשאית` means *delegation of
  authority, covertly*, where [§1](#1-product-overview) and [§3.3](#33-delegation-visibility-model-depth--1)
  say *delegating work, confidentially* — `חשאית` is *clandestine*, not `חסויה`. Both are load-bearing
  concepts whose **Hebrew rendering is fixed nowhere in this document**, and the second sits on the surface
  [D26](#7-open-decisions) governs, so it may not be reworded before D26 closes. **The field label:**
  `אזור פעילות` (*area of activity*) against *Location* — a real mismatch with two defensible repairs,
  since the schema treats location and travel radius as separate concepts while this field's placeholder is
  a city and a region. **Three trade-list pairs:** `ריצוף וחיפוי` (*flooring and cladding*) against *Tiling
  and flooring*, where `חיפוי` is cladding and the English is also internally redundant; `משאבות חול` (*sand
  pumps*, equipment) against *Sand pumping* (an activity); and `קבלן שלד` / `קבלן לעבודות פיתוח ותשתיות`,
  the only two entries in a list of **trades** that name a **person**. The trade list is owner-approved
  (`dc54ec8`), which is why none of it was touched. **Two smaller inconsistencies ride along:** `ממ"ד` uses
  an ASCII quote while `צמ״ה` uses a gershayim in the same list, and the two field hints disagree about
  trailing full stops in both languages. Separately, the availability options use masculine-only Hebrew
  (`מקבל`) where the same screen's consent line uses the neutral `מסכים/ה`; that wording is settled
  terminology per the owner and is listed here for the record only.
- **`register.html` claims end-to-end encryption for what is TLS.** The security footnote reads
  *`TLS · end-to-end encrypted session`* — transport encryption is not E2EE, and the claim is wrong in
  **both** languages, which makes it a copy-accuracy problem rather than a translation one. The 2026-08-26
  pass corrected only the Hebrew mistranslation inside it (`הפעלה`, *activation*, → `חיבור`) and
  **deliberately left the claim standing**, because weakening or removing a security statement is an owner
  decision rather than a QA one. The element is `aria-hidden="true"`, so it is decorative to assistive
  technology and misleading only to people reading the screen. `login.css`'s sibling note carries similar
  wording and was not opened.
- ~~**The password-reset flow has two React screens and no server behind either of them, and it is now the earliest-biting gap in the project**~~
  ✅ **Closed the same day (`bc6f032`, `f5fd3e9`).** Both endpoints exist, the mail-transport decision
  is made — **Nodemailer over Brevo SMTP** — and both screens make real requests. What the original
  entry asked for is what was built: an answer that does not differ by whether the account exists,
  a single-use token stored as a hash with an expiry, and an error contract for a dead link. **Two
  things it did not ask for and were added anyway**, because the enumeration guarantee needs them:
  the email is dispatched **off the request path**, so a known address cannot answer measurably
  slower than an unknown one; and the limiter added later was checked to keep the two answers
  identical *while limiting*. **Deployment still owes the Brevo credentials** — an unconfigured
  server runs in log mode, which cannot be mistaken for working. Original entry below.
  (2026-08-29). Forgot password and Reset password were migrated (`2c225ca`) and **send no request**, because there is nothing to send one to: `auth.routes.ts` mounts `/register`, `/login` and `/refresh` and nothing else; a grep for `forgot`, `reset-password`, `passwordReset`, `nodemailer` or `mail` across `Backend/src`, `package.json` and `requests/` returns nothing; and **five candidate paths were probed against the running API and every one answered `404 ROUTE_NOT_FOUND`**. So there is no endpoint, **no reset token of any kind**, and no mail transport or provider. The screens therefore advance to their approved steps and each states that nothing was sent or changed — the same honest build-state note Register's success panel used to carry. **Two things were deliberately not invented, and both would have been contracts:** posting to an address that does not exist, and reading a `?token=` off the URL. Nothing anywhere names that parameter, so choosing one would have fixed the token mechanism from a frontend branch. **What this owes**, and it is a decision before it is code: a mail transport and a provider ([D3](#7-open-decisions)'s neighbour, and the Decision Map has called this the earliest-biting open decision since it was drawn), then a `POST` that always answers the same way whether or not the account exists, a single-use token with a stored hash and an expiry, and the error contract for a token that is expired, spent or unknown. **The anti-enumeration half is already proven on the client**: a real account and an unknown address produce the identical confirmation, measured rather than asserted.
- **Two [§3.5](#35-shared-ui-rules-apply-to-every-screen) rule 6 violations moved into the React tree unchanged, and that was deliberate** (2026-08-29). `.back-link` at **11px** and `.form-hint` at **12px** are both under the 15px supporting-text floor, and [§8](#8-known-issues--risks) already recorded them as *"a rule violation as they stand"* needing *"their own pass with those two screens open in a browser."* The password-reset migration copied them into `src/features/password-reset/password-reset.css` **as they are**, because this was a migration and the document had already deferred the correction to a pass of its own. They are now in one small file rather than buried in the shared auth stylesheet, which is the only thing that improved. **They are still owed**, and the two screens they belong to are now React screens, so the pass no longer has to wait for anything.
- **The React app has one document where the prototypes had six, and their stylesheets disagree** (2026-08-29). Each static screen was its own page, so each could redefine `:root`, `.btn`, `.form-input` and `.lang-switch` in its own terms — and they do: `.btn` has an 8px gap in `profile.css` and 10px in `register.css`, and the field heights and shadows differ too. Imported the ordinary way into one single-page application, whichever stylesheet loaded last would silently repaint the others, and the failure would look like a design regression rather than an import order. **The resolution keeps the CSS byte-identical:** `src/styles/useScreenStylesheet.ts` applies a screen's sheet while that screen is mounted and removes it after, reference-counted so two screens sharing one — My profile and Edit profile both load `profile.css` — do not pull it out from under each other. The CSS arrives as a string in the bundle rather than a second request, so no frame is painted unstyled. **The alternative was rewriting every selector to be scoped**, which would have meant editing ~2,500 lines of approved visual design to solve an import problem. **The thing to remember:** this constraint arrives the moment a second screen with its own stylesheet is migrated, not when the first is.
- **The reset-token storage in the code and in `docs/database-design.html` disagree, deliberately, and the schema page has not been updated** (2026-08-29). The page designs `users.security.resetTokenHash` + `users.security.resetTokenExpiresAt` with a sparse index, and `refreshToken.model.ts` cites that design in a comment. The code instead uses a **`passwordresettokens` collection** mirroring [[refreshtokens]]. **The reason is what those two fields cannot express:** a reset token has a lifecycle — issued, superseded, spent, expired — and a hash-plus-expiry pair cannot tell a spent link from an absent one, cannot record that a newer request replaced an older one, and puts security state on the permanent identity document. **The one field of that `security` sub-document that IS implemented is `passwordChangedAt`**, exactly as the page designs it, because the page's own reasoning for it — *"lets the auth middleware reject JWTs issued before a password change"* — is precisely what it now does. So the page is right about one field, superseded on two, and silent on a whole collection. Golden rule 3 says that file changes in the same sitting as the model; it did not, because the sittings that built this were scoped to backend documentation. **It is the largest single piece of documentation debt this project currently carries**, and it is worse than an omission: a stale schema page will be trusted.
- **Rate limiting exists now, and its numbers are engineering defaults rather than approved values** (2026-08-29, `6a06d32`). Login 10 / 15 min, Register 10 / hour, forgot-password 5 / 15 min, reset-password 10 / 15 min, all per caller IP and never by the submitted email — keying a reset limiter on the address in the body would let anyone spend a chosen person's recovery quota. `429 TOO_MANY_REQUESTS` is **the first 429 in the project**; it answers through the same `{ code, message }` contract as everything else rather than the library's own body. Three limits of the implementation, stated rather than discovered later: the counters are **in memory**, so each dyno limits independently and a restart clears them; the values are **not env-tunable**, so changing them is a code change; and `trust proxy` is enabled **only in production**, which is right for Heroku's single hop and wrong for any other topology — without it every caller behind a proxy shares one key. `POST /auth/refresh` is deliberately unlimited, and ordinary application traffic is untouched.
- **Making a stateless token revocable costs a database read on every authenticated request** (2026-08-29, `d0a441e`, `6a06d32`). `requireAccessToken` now does one indexed lookup projected to `status` and `security.passwordChangedAt`. That is the price of the guarantee, and there is no cheaper honest version — there is no way to know a token is stale without asking. If it ever matters the answer is a short-TTL cache keyed by user id, **not** a weaker check. **And one second of granularity is accepted knowingly:** a JWT's `iat` is whole seconds, so `passwordChangedAt` is stored truncated to match and a token minted in the *same second* as a reset survives. Rounding the other way was tried first and the verification caught it rejecting the token Login mints moments later — a legitimate sign-in failing, which is the worse of the two.
- **The employee lifecycle is built and has no screen** (2026-08-29, `19ec45b`). An owner can open a seat, see who is waiting and approve them — over the API only. ~~Employee management is **not on the approved 36-screen list**, so no design exists for it~~ — **corrected 2026-08-29: it is a defined product feature**, owning company member management, invitations, employee status, pending approvals, individual and bulk approval and member details. What is missing is its **integration** with these four routes, not a design for it, and until that lands the flow is complete but reachable only over the API. Two rules that ride with it and are recorded rather than coded: **an employee must not appear in Browse** (Browse selects people holding an `owner` membership, and Browse is not built), and **leaving a company has no path** — `inactive` sits in the enum with no endpoint reaching it.
- **One employee Register restriction rests on structure rather than an explicit rule, and it is the one to revisit** (2026-08-29). Two fields are refused when `standing` is `employee`. **`availability` has an explicit closed rule** — [D14](#7-open-decisions) makes it the organization's work availability, *"controlled by the execution contractor / company owner"*, with *"deliberately no per-user copy and no per-user override"*, and says it *"must not be reinterpreted as the personal availability of each employee"*. **`officePhone` does not.** [D27](#7-open-decisions) puts it on the company, and an employee registration writes no company, so the field has nowhere to go: accepting and discarding it is what `specialtyOther`'s `forbidden()` exists to prevent, and writing it to the employer's company would let somebody **not yet approved** edit that company's record. Refusing was the only option that took no decision — but the decision it is standing in for is real and unasked: **may an employee propose company data before they are approved?** Answering it either way is one line. Recorded because the first version of this rule was argued from the wrong thing entirely — that the invitation is matched on three values, which is about identity, not about what a person may supply.
- **A `.env` that predates a variable fails silently, and `TERMS_VERSION` is the one that matters.**
  Required variables crash the server when absent, and defaulted ones reveal a wrong value through
  behaviour — `TERMS_VERSION` does neither. It defaults to `draft`, and that value is copied verbatim
  into `users.termsAcceptances[]`, which [D25](#7-open-decisions) makes **append-only precisely so an
  acceptance stays provable** — so a wrong entry can never be corrected. Harmless today (no Terms
  document exists, `draft` is honest); wrong from the moment one is published, on any deployment or
  developer machine whose `.env` was created earlier. The durable fix is to drop the `.default('draft')`
  in `config/env.ts` the day Terms ship, turning a silent gap into a refusal to start.
  **The lesson was applied to the variables added on 2026-08-29.** `FRONTEND_URL` is **required**,
  because a reset email carrying the wrong link is worse than a server that will not boot; and the
  four SMTP variables are **all-or-nothing**, because three of four is a deployment that looks
  configured and fails on the first send. Neither can fail silently. The cost is the one this item
  already names: a `.env` created before 2026-08-29 does not start until `FRONTEND_URL` is added.

## 9. Working notes / scratch

_Free space for in-progress thinking, links, snippets, "remember to…" notes. Promote anything important
into a proper section or the logs below._

## 10. Bug & Issue Log

> Every problem solved goes here so we never solve it twice. Newest at top.
>
> **Format:**
> ```
> ### [YYYY-MM-DD] Short title
> - Symptom: what went wrong / what you observed
> - Cause: the actual root cause
> - Fix: what resolved it
> - Notes: anything to remember / how to avoid it next time
> ```

### [2026-08-30] Google Places autocomplete returns zero options in a headless run, intermittently — OPEN, under investigation

**Symptom.** Browser suites that drive the structured location field see the Places autocomplete
list stay empty: `0 options`, `none`. In one run the browser console also carried
`Access to fetch at 'https://places.googleapis.com/v1/places:autocomplete' … blocked by CORS
policy: No 'Access-Control-Allow-Origin' header` and `net::ERR_FAILED`.

**Suites affected.** `verify:integrated` — the four checks in *Browse Places autocomplete* and
*Places localization* — and `verify:create-task-ui` on one occasion, through its shared registration
helper.

**Observed sequence.** First appeared with 5 failures across `create-task-ui` and `integrated` while
ten browser suites ran back to back; both suites then passed clean on an immediate re-run
(66/0 and 64/0). It returned later the same day: `integrated` aborted early once with the
registration helper timing out, then scored 60/4, then passed **64/0** when run in isolation with
nothing else competing. It did **not** reappear on the post-merge run.

**What is not the cause.** The API key is fine. Probed directly with the browser's own referer
(`http://localhost:5173/`) it answered `5 suggestions` on three consecutive calls while the browser
run was failing. Without a referer it correctly answers `API_KEY_HTTP_REFERRER_BLOCKED`, which is
the restriction working as configured, not a fault.

**Working hypothesis, unproven.** Rate or quota pressure from many headless suites calling Places in
quick succession, or a transient network failure inside headless Chromium. **The product feature is
not classified as broken** — no evidence yet says a real user is affected.

**No product change was made in response.** The key was not rotated and the Places integration was
not altered.

**New consequence worth knowing (2026-08-30).** Since Register became two steps, a Places failure is
no longer worth one assertion: the city never fills, so Step 1 never completes, `#register-next`
stays disabled, and the suite can time out on the click and lose everything after it. A suite that
used to degrade now aborts.

**Next step.** Investigate later with request-level logging from inside the browser context, and
consider a retry-with-backoff in the shared registration helper rather than in product code.

### [2026-08-30] A grant edit answered an invitation on the invitee's behalf

**Problem.** `projectGrantRepository.upsert` wrote `status` on every call. Editing a pending person's
permissions from the central Permissions screen therefore flipped their row from `invited` to `active`
— accepting an invitation *for* them, silently, from a screen that is not about invitations at all.

**Fix.** `status` is insert-only: an existing row keeps the status it earned, and only the invitee's own
accept or decline moves it. **Engineering correction.** Asserted directly — editing a pending person's
permissions leaves the invitation open and still grants them no read on the project.

### [2026-08-30] Re-adding a removed or declined person put them back silently

**Problem.** Granting permissions to somebody whose membership was `removed` or `declined` wrote the
grant onto the existing row. They were back on the project without ever being asked.

**Fix.** A grant against such a row now creates an **invitation** they answer themselves. There is exactly
one way onto a project, and nobody is quietly reinstated. **Engineering correction**, protecting the
consent half of the closed invitation model.

### [2026-08-30] Cancelling a project orphaned its memberships and open invitations

**Problem.** Pre-start cancellation hard-deletes the project ([D24](#7-open-decisions)) but deleted nothing
else. Membership rows outlived the project they pointed at, and open invitations stayed on the invitee's
screen offering a project that no longer existed.

**Fix.** Cancellation takes the project's memberships and invitations with it. **Engineering correction.**

### [2026-08-30] A declined grant rendered as a live one

**Problem.** The central Permissions screen showed a `declined` row with editable permission checkboxes,
a working reduce control and no chip. It described an authority nobody held, and a refusal looked
identical to a live grant.

**Fix.** `declined` carries its own chip and is read-only, exactly like `removed`. **Engineering
correction**, found while reviewing the grant row after the Members batch introduced the new status.

### [2026-08-30] A My Tasks filter could be set but never cleared

**Problem.** The task filters were built from the shared `SelectField`, which renders its placeholder as
`<option disabled>`. That is right for a form — a required select must stay invalid until chosen — and
wrong for a filter, where "all" is a real choice. Once a status filter was set there was no way back to
the full list.

**Fix.** The filters use raw selects with an enabled "all" option, the pattern the Browse rail already
used. **Engineering correction**, found by a browser test that could not restore its own starting state.

### [2026-08-30] A long project name overflowed the viewport by 2110px on mobile

**Problem.** `.profile__title` had no `overflow-wrap`, so a project name arriving as one unbroken run of
characters — which free text can be — pushed the Project Dashboard sideways at 390px.

**Fix.** `overflow-wrap: anywhere` on the title, scoped to the screen's own stylesheet. **Engineering
correction**, found by the responsive sweep rather than by reading the CSS.

### [2026-08-30] The project lifecycle port existed, answered truthfully, and was connected to nothing

**Problem.** `ProjectExecutionPort.hasFirstTaskStarted` became real when the Tasks domain landed, but
`deriveStatus` still read a stored `projects.startedAt` that nothing ever wrote. A project whose first task
had genuinely started went on reporting `planned`, and I reported that wiring it was "a lifecycle write not
in this batch" — treating a **closed** rule as an open question. The rule was already written verbatim in
the lifecycle service's own comment.

**Fix.** Status derives from both halves of the closed rule: the configured start date has arrived **AND**
a real task has actually started through an explicit Start. A task started before the project's start date
does not start the project, and that is asserted. **Engineering correction of my own reporting error — not a
new owner decision.** The lifecycle rule itself was never reopened.

### [2026-08-30] `projects.startedAt` removed as a redundant second source of truth

**Problem.** The field was designed as a cache of "the closed start rule has fired". Once the Tasks domain
could answer that question exactly, a stored copy could only drift from the tasks it summarised — and
nothing in the application ever wrote it, so it was already stale by construction.

**Fix.** Removed from the schema; the fact is asked of the Tasks domain per read, with a batch form
(`startedProjectIds`) so a page of projects costs one query rather than one per row. **Engineering
correction.** Completion was deliberately **not** given the same treatment: the closed rule requires the
work finished to quality as well as every task closed, and the model holds no representation of the quality
half, so `completedAt` remains the stored record that both were satisfied.

### [2026-08-29] A filter that was working correctly was reported as broken, and both halves of that are true
> **The finding in six lines, because this one will be asked about again.**
> 1. **The filter logic is correct.** Every threshold case behaves as specified.
> 2. **Filtering is server-side.** The client sends `minRating` and renders exactly what comes back; it filters nothing locally.
> 3. **Thresholds are inclusive.** A contractor whose average is exactly 3.0 meets `minRating=3`; exactly 4.0 meets `minRating=4`.
> 4. **Unrated contractors are excluded**, and are never counted as a zero — that is the approved rule, not a bug.
> 5. **The product has no real ratings**, because `RatingEligibilityPort` **intentionally denies rating creation** until the real Tasks / Completed Work domain exists.
> 6. **Therefore the filter is currently starved of real production data, not logically broken.**

- Symptom: owner manual QA marked **Minimum Rating** as FAILED. Choosing any number of stars emptied the result list.
- Cause: **not the filter.** Traced end to end — React control → query parameter → JOI → aggregation → `summaryForMany` → the `>= minRating` comparison → cursor → DTO → rendered cards — and proved on the **result set** with six controlled contractors at `unrated`, `2.5`, `3.0`, `3.5`, `4.0` and `5.0`. Every case behaves correctly, including exact-threshold equality, all four filter combinations, and pagination with no duplicate and nothing eligible skipped. **The real cause is upstream: no rating can exist.** `POST /api/ratings` answers `403 RATING_NOT_ELIGIBLE` to every caller because `RatingEligibilityPort` returns `false` for everybody until the Tasks / Completed Work domain is built — so the working database held **zero ratings across 81 active contractors**. Every contractor is unrated, an unrated contractor correctly fails every floor, and therefore the correct answer to *any* minimum rating is an empty list.
- Fix: none to the filter — there was nothing to fix, and changing it would have meant counting an unrated contractor as a zero, which the approved rule forbids. What was fixed is the **silence**: the empty state now names the floor that emptied the list and says an unrated contractor is not in it, instead of the generic *no contractors match these filters*. Two regression suites were added that assert the **result set**, never the query parameter: `npm run verify:min-rating` (46 checks, API) and `npm run verify:min-rating-ui` (34 checks, real browser, read off the rendered cards).
- Notes: **"the filter works" and "the filter is usable" are different claims, and only the first was ever true.** The previous pass reported `Minimum rating — kept`, which asserted neither and was the actual reporting failure. A correct empty result is indistinguishable from a broken one unless the screen says which filter emptied it — that is now the rule this screen follows. **This filter stays effectively unusable until Tasks / Completed Work lands**, however correct it is.

### [2026-08-29] A public profile advertised two picture URLs, and neither one could ever load
- Symptom: an embedded Public Profile showed initials where an avatar should be, and a placeholder icon where a completed-work photo should be, for a contractor who had uploaded both.
- Cause: two separate mistakes in one DTO. The avatar URL was built as `/api/browse/contractors/:id/avatar` for a **route that was never registered** — the string existed, the endpoint did not. The work-entry photo URL was built as `/api/users/me/assets/:id`, the **owner-only** asset route, so a viewer looking at somebody else's profile was correctly refused by a route that was never meant to serve them.
- Fix: registered both routes on the browse module, scoped by the block list **and** by the subject's ownership of the asset, and pointed the DTO at the new work-image route.
- Notes: a URL in a DTO is a claim, and nothing type-checks it against the router. Both were invisible because the screen degraded politely — an `<img>` that 404s just shows the fallback. The test that catches this asks the profile for its URLs and then **fetches them**, which is the only version that proves anything.

### [2026-08-29] A control that discarded what a person typed, and a note admitting it
- Symptom: choosing heavy equipment on Edit profile did nothing. A hint under the picker said the choice would not be kept.
- Cause: [D14](#7-open-decisions)'s heavy-equipment half had no schema, so the picker held local state only. The honest note was the previous pass's deliberate choice under *flag the gap rather than drop the control*.
- Fix: on owner instruction, built `users.heavyEquipment` — an array of the ten codes the approved screens already offered — with the rule that the list is cleared whenever the `heavy_equipment` trade is not among the specialties a patch leaves behind. The note is deleted because it is no longer true.
- Notes: **flagging a gap and shipping a control that discards input are not the same thing**, and the owner drew that line here: production-facing UI may not knowingly throw work away, whatever the note says. The shape is still the narrowest of D14's three and awaits ratification — building it did not close the decision.

### [2026-08-29] Clearing a saved location and pressing Save looked exactly like a successful save
- Symptom: a person cleared their location, pressed Save, saw no error, and the old location came back.
- Cause: the patch builder skipped a `null` place (`if (values.place !== null && …)`), so an empty field sent **nothing**. The save then succeeded — it had other fields to write — re-read the server, and refilled the form with the stored value. Every visible signal said saved.
- Fix: the form now computes its required fields, Save is blocked while any is empty, every one is marked with the established touched-and-invalid treatment, and the strip says the change was not submitted. Only Cancel restores stored values.
- Notes: **"send only what changed" and "a cleared field is a change" are in tension**, and the first quietly won. The four states a form can end in — saved, cancelled, invalid, server failure — must be visibly different; here three of them looked like the first.

### [2026-08-29] An error message that no stylesheet could ever show
- Symptom: Login gave an invalid email address a red border and a hard-hat icon, and never said what was wrong. Register, on the same design system, wrote a full message.
- Cause: two failures that hid each other. `LoginForm` never passed the `error` prop its field components already accepted; and `login.css` declared `.field-error { display: none }` but **never defined `.field-error--visible`**, the class those components add. So even a passed message would have stayed hidden.
- Fix: Login passes the same messages through the same components, and `login.css` carries register's error block verbatim. Its in-field hard-hat also moved from a physical `right` to `inset-inline-end`, where it had been sitting over the text in Hebrew.
- Notes: a dead CSS class is invisible to a typechecker and to a screenshot of a valid form. The check that found it asks for the message's **height**, because a rule that never matches and a rule that matches nothing look identical in the DOM.

### [2026-08-29] A person appeared in their own Browse results
- Symptom: searching Browse returned the searcher.
- Cause: `browse.service.ts` excluded the block list and nothing else. The viewer's own id was never in it.
- Fix: the viewer joins the exclusion, server-side, guarded by `Types.ObjectId.isValid` so a non-id caller cannot make the service throw.
- Notes: the guard is not defensive noise — the first version crashed an existing adapter test that passes a fake viewer id, which is how the fragility surfaced. Two existing assertions expected the viewer *among* the results; both were rewritten to the new rule and one gained a stronger check, rather than being relaxed.

### [2026-08-29] A real account showed a stranger's company, phones and rating, and the data was never wrong
- Symptom: a freshly registered account displayed `כאכון בנייה בע״מ`, `04-8123456`, `052-555-0123`, city `חיפה`, rating `4.6` and a project called `מגדלי הצפון` — none of it entered by the person, and the company name they typed was nowhere on screen.
- Cause: `representativeProfile()` in `features/profile/profileModel.ts` returned a fabricated profile with only name and email taken from the real user. Its own comment asserted *"The API has no profile endpoint"* — true when written, false once the profile backend landed. Both screens called it and made **zero** HTTP requests.
- Fix: deleted the function and the whole `REPRESENTATIVE` block; wired both screens to `GET /users/me`, `PATCH /users/me` and `PATCH /companies/me`.
- Notes: **the database always held the right values** — a controlled re-registration proved every field stored exactly as typed. A stale comment asserting a missing endpoint outlived the endpoint by weeks. When a module claims a backend does not exist, that claim needs a date and an owner, or it silently becomes a lie.

### [2026-08-29] The filter rail slid over the panel beneath it, but only while scrolling
- Symptom: owner QA reported the basic filter rail rising over the Advanced Filters area on Browse. Automated checks had reported no overlap.
- Cause: below 1180px a panel is promoted to `grid-column: 1 / -1`, which starts a **second grid row**. Its sticky siblings in row one stayed sticky and travelled down over it. Measured at 1100px: a 268×434px overlap at `scrollY=600`.
- Fix: once any panel spans the row, its siblings stop being sticky; the rail is also capped to the viewport with its own scroll. `.adv-panel` deliberately gets **no** `overflow`, which would clip the Places dropdown inside it.
- Notes: **the earlier checks measured three viewports at `scrollY=0`.** A sticky bug cannot exist at scroll zero. `verify:layout` now scans nine scroll positions across six widths in both languages, and exists because CSS inspection cannot prove this class of fault.

### [2026-08-29] A card's link logged an authenticated person out
- Symptom: *Manage in My network* on a contractor card sent a signed-in user to the login screen.
- Cause: a router `<Link to="/network">`, but `/network` is not a route — the catch-all redirects unknown paths to `/login`.
- Fix: the same `#` treatment the navbar already uses for unmigrated destinations.
- Notes: it recurred as a design question one day later for the account menu's **Settings** entry, and was answered the same way: **disabled with a reason beats a link to a route that does not exist.**

### [2026-08-29] Google Places answered in English while the interface was in Hebrew
- Symptom: place suggestions came back as `Haifa` with the app in Hebrew, and stale results from the previous language stayed on screen after switching.
- Cause: neither the autocomplete nor the details request sent a locale, and the search effect did not depend on the language.
- Fix: `languageCode` (plus `regionCode: IL`) on both calls, taken from `LanguageContext`; the language is in the effect's dependencies and results are cleared the moment it changes.
- Notes: verified live both ways — `חיפה` in Hebrew, `Haifa` in English, and `חיפה` again on switching back.

### [2026-08-29] A person's name appeared to translate itself
- Symptom: the navbar name changed when the interface language was switched.
- Cause: **the static prototype**, not React. `screens/*.html` hard-code a bilingual pair — `<span class="he-text">בר כאכון</span><span class="en-text">Bar Kakon</span>` — and hide one with CSS. React renders one stored value with `dir="auto"`; measured across four language switches it never changed.
- Fix: none needed in React. A regression check now asserts the stored name and initials across `he → en → he`.
- Notes: a worked example of stale static behaviour being read as current product behaviour. It is also the clearest argument for deleting the static screens once the React ones are approved.

### [2026-08-29] Two committed `node_modules` symlinks, from a `.gitignore` that could not match them
- Symptom: a feature commit contained `Backend/node_modules` and `Frontend/node_modules`.
- Cause: worktrees were given symlinked `node_modules`, and the root `.gitignore` lists `node_modules/` **with a trailing slash** — which matches a directory, not a symlink.
- Fix: amended out of the commit and force-pushed before the branch was used; a local `.git/info/exclude` prevents recurrence.
- Notes: this is the **second** time it happened in this project. The durable fix is a bare `node_modules` line in the tracked `.gitignore`, recorded in [§8](#8-known-issues--risks).

### [2026-08-29] Closing a one-second security window opened a worse one, and a test caught it
- Symptom: a password reset must kill Access Tokens issued before it. A JWT's `iat` is **whole seconds**, and `passwordChangedAt` is a millisecond `Date`, so the two have to be compared in one unit. Rounding the stamp **up** to the next second was the obvious choice: it leaves no window in which a pre-reset token is accepted. The verification then failed on `Access Token B, minted after the reset, works`.
- Cause: **rounding up also rejects the token Login mints moments later.** After a reset the person is sent to Login; if they sign in inside the same second, that brand-new token's `iat` floors *below* the rounded-up stamp and is refused. The test signs in immediately, so it hit every time — a real user with a password manager would too.
- Fix: store `passwordChangedAt` **truncated** to the second and compare strictly. A token from any earlier second is dead; one minted in the same second as the reset survives.
- Notes: **both directions have a one-second flaw and they are not equally bad.** Rounding up fails a legitimate sign-in — visible, reproducible, and a support call. Truncating leaves a sub-second window an attacker can only use if they already hold the password *and* hit the same second as the victim's reset. The lesson is not "truncate"; it is that when two clocks have different resolutions, **write down which way the error falls and pick the direction that fails safe for the user**. The alternative — a custom millisecond claim — changes the token contract to buy a sub-second, which is not a trade worth making.

### [2026-08-29] A test suite that spends a rate limit reports the limiter as a bug
- Symptom: minutes after the auth limiters shipped, `verify:password-reset` started failing on assertions that had nothing to do with limiting — `wrong password and unknown email stay unified` reporting a 429 body, `two rows exist, and exactly one is still live` reporting one row. Every failure looked like a regression in the feature under test.
- Cause: **the script spends a large share of the auth budget, and the counters are per process.** Run twice inside one window and the second run meets its own first run. Nothing was broken; the suite was measuring a server it had already exhausted.
- Fix: the HTTP helper now throws the moment any request answers 429, with a message saying the script needs a freshly started server. One guard, every endpoint, instead of a special case per call. The dedicated `verify:rate-limit` script is the one that *expects* 429.
- Notes: **a test that shares mutable server state with itself is not repeatable, and the failure it produces points at the wrong place.** Worth remembering the moment any limiter, quota or counter is added: decide immediately whether the suites reset it, avoid it, or refuse to run without a clean one. The same class of mistake produced two more harness bugs the same day — two test accounts built from `first.toLowerCase()` colliding on one email, and a "is this a JWT" check that matched any string with two dots, so a stored user object containing `migration.check@example.com` looked like a second token. In all three the code was correct and the test was wrong; each was found because an assertion nearby was specific enough to contradict it.

### [2026-08-29] Six stylesheets that never met were about to overwrite each other
- Symptom: found before it shipped, by reading rather than by breakage. Migrating Login and the two profile screens meant a single-page application would hold `register.css`, `login.css`, `profile.css`, `my-profile.css` and `edit-profile.css` at once, and every one of them defines `:root`, `.btn`, `.form-input` and `.lang-switch`. The values differ — `.btn` gap 8px in `profile.css` against 10px in `register.css`, different field heights, different shadows — so whichever import came last would have repainted the others.
- Cause: **the prototypes were separate documents, and a stylesheet that owns `:root` is only safe while nothing else is on the page.** Nothing in the CSS is wrong; the assumption underneath it stopped holding the moment a second screen was migrated. The first migration could not surface it, because one screen never collides with itself.
- Fix: `src/styles/useScreenStylesheet.ts` — a screen's sheet is applied while that screen is mounted and removed when it unmounts, reference-counted so My profile and Edit profile can both hold `profile.css` without either pulling it from the other. The CSS is imported as a string (`?inline`) so it is already in the bundle: no second request, and no frame painted unstyled. **Not one declaration was edited.**
- Notes: the alternative was scoping every selector under a per-screen root, which is roughly 2,500 lines of approved visual design rewritten to solve an import-order problem — and every rewritten line is a chance to change the design by accident. **The check that proves it worked is not "the screens look right"**: it is a computed-style comparison of each static screen against its React counterpart in both languages, which reports zero differences on Login, Register, Forgot password and Reset password. Expect this constraint on screen two of any migration, never on screen one.

### [2026-08-29] The English profile screens had always rendered in the Hebrew font, and fixing it moved a width
- Symptom: [§8](#8-known-issues--risks) had recorded that `body` computes Heebo while English is showing, because `body` is an ancestor of `.app` and the English swap redefined `--font-sans` on `.app`. Fixed in the React migration by moving `direction` and both font variables to `<html>` — and the static-versus-React comparison then reported `.profile__head-text` at **818px** before and **881px** after, which reads exactly like a layout regression.
- Cause: **`max-width: 92ch`.** The `ch` unit resolves against the computed font, so the same declaration measures one width in Heebo and another in Inter. The width did not change; the font under it did, which is the bug being fixed rather than a new one.
- Fix: none needed. What the pass did need was `.app` losing its own `direction: rtl` — with direction on the root, a container rule at higher specificity would have pinned every English screen back to RTL, and the language toggle would have swapped the words and not the layout.
- Notes: **a diff that reports a width change after a font change is not evidence of a layout change**, and the way to tell them apart is to look for a font-relative unit before reaching for the layout. Two of the differences in that comparison run were this; the rest were hidden `he-field` twins measuring zero in the static page. **The eight static screens listed in [§8](#8-known-issues--risks) still carry the original defect** — the React fix does not travel to them.

### [2026-08-28] The React client's Register request answered `ROUTE_NOT_FOUND`
- Symptom: the newly wired React Register screen submitted a correct request and the API answered **`404 ROUTE_NOT_FOUND — Route POST /api/auth/register not found`**. The client had just been verified end to end against a stub, so the obvious reading was that the client was wrong.
- Cause: **the endpoint had never been on the branch the running server was started from.** Register was built on `feature/register-screen.backend` and that branch was **not merged into `develop`**. `develop`'s `auth.routes.ts` mounted `/login` and `/refresh` and nothing else, so Express fell through to `notFoundHandler` — which produced the message verbatim. Nothing was broken; a route simply did not exist yet.
- Fix (`9ee8b43`): merged `feature/register-screen.backend` into `develop` after resolving the two documentation conflicts in `develop`'s favour. **No client code was changed**, and no workaround was added — the owner's instruction was explicit that the React screen must not be adapted around a missing route, which was the right call: any accommodation would have had to be removed an hour later.
- Notes: **the diagnosis was worth confirming rather than assuming, and confirming it took one request.** `POST /api/auth/login` against the *same* server answered `401 INVALID_CREDENTIALS` — which proves the process was the API, was routing correctly, and was simply missing one route. A 404 from your own API has two very different causes — a wrong URL, or a route that does not exist on the running build — and one request to a neighbouring route separates them. **The second lesson is about which branch is running.** A server started before a merge keeps serving the old build until it is restarted, so "is the code on the branch" and "is the code in the process" are two questions. Both were checked here: the route in `origin/develop`'s source, and a live `201` from a server started from the merged tree.

### [2026-08-26] A Hebrew placeholder stayed Hebrew in the English form
- Symptom: on `register.html` the Full name field showed `ישראל ישראלי` as its placeholder **in both languages**. Everything around it swapped — the label, the hint, the button — so the form read as properly bilingual and the one attribute quietly did not participate. The English screen asked an English-speaking contractor for their name and gave them a Hebrew example to copy.
- Cause: **a placeholder is an attribute, and the `.he-text` / `.en-text` span trick can only swap element content.** The screen already knew this — the Location field and the free-text trade field are each duplicated into `.he-field` / `.en-field` twins for exactly this reason, with a comment saying so — but Full name was written as a single input and never revisited. The same class of defect as the duplicated trade `<select>`, which [§3.4](#34-bilingual--bidirectional-ui-hebrew-rtl-default--english-ltr) records as the cost of the duplicate-markup pattern.
- Fix (`c402429`): Full name became `.he-field` / `.en-field` twins carrying `ישראל ישראלי` and *John Smith*, following the Location pattern already in the file rather than inventing a third mechanism. Its `id="fullname"` was dropped, since two elements cannot share one id, so the label lost its `for` and each input took an `aria-label` — again what Location already does. The `:has(#fullname:placeholder-shown)` line in the submit-gating list was **deleted rather than repointed**: the generic `.form-group > .he-field` / `.en-field` rules directly below it already cover any twinned field in the active language only, so the specific rule was dead weight the moment the field was twinned.
- Notes: **the reusable check is not "is every string translated" — it is "is every user-visible *attribute* translated."** Content is easy to audit by eye because it is on the screen in both languages during review; `placeholder`, `aria-label`, `title` and `alt` are invisible to that review and each needs its own mechanism. Two English-only `aria-label`s on the same screen (both password reveal buttons, and the feature list) were found by grepping for `aria-label=` rather than by reading the page, which is the way to find them. **Deleting a now-dead `:has()` line is part of the fix, not tidying** — leaving it would have left a selector referencing an id that no longer exists, which reads as intentional to the next person. The wider debt is unchanged: this pattern needs a duplicate for every language-dependent attribute, and React collapses it into one string per render ([D10](#7-open-decisions)).

### [2026-08-26] A new marker shipped at 12.5px, three sittings after the size floor was written down
- Symptom: the *Optional* / `לא חובה` marker added beside the two new phone labels was authored at **12.5px**, below both the 14px label reference in [§3.5](#35-shared-ui-rules-apply-to-every-screen) rule 3 and the 15px supporting-text floor in rule 6. Caught in review before it was documented, but after it had been committed.
- Cause: it was sized by **analogy to the nearest-looking element instead of by role**. `.brand-logo__badge` is a 12px pill and the new marker is also a pill, so it inherited that size. But the badge says *Beta* — decoration — while this marker is the only thing on the screen telling a contractor that a field may be left empty. **The 2026-08-25 audit of `register.css` had already drawn exactly this line** — *"everything below 14.5px there is a mono chip or control label, not reading text"* — and adding a 12.5px element that **is** reading text would have quietly falsified that finding in the file it was measured against.
- Fix (`42b8a27`): 14px, matching the `.form-label` it sits inside. It stays visually secondary through `--text-mute` and the pill outline rather than through size, which is what rule 6 explicitly permits.
- Notes: **this is the fourth instance of one failure** — after the 2026-08-19 label sweep, the 2026-08-20 card-value pass and the 2026-08-25 Login pass — and it is the first where the rule was already written down and numbered when the code was authored. So the floor being a number did not prevent it; what would have is asking *"is this text the user has to read?"* before choosing a size, rather than *"what does the element beside it use?"*. **Visual similarity is not role similarity**, and a pill is a shape, not a category. Worth noting the failure arrived in a change whose whole other half was a readability QA pass.

### [2026-08-25] The first fix moved the toggle and left the page unmirrored — the request was the page
- Symptom: **this is the second attempt at one request, and the first attempt did not satisfy it.** The owner asked for the *Login screen* to switch direction with the language — Hebrew RTL, English LTR, structure and alignment included, explicitly *"do not only translate the text while leaving the Hebrew layout direction in place"*. The fix below (`4e1cf33`) treated it as a language-pill placement problem, moved the pill between corners, and shipped. The owner reported it as still wrong, and it was: `.auth-layout`'s `grid-template-columns: 44% 1fr` kept the navy brand panel on the **physical left in both languages**, so Hebrew rendered translated strings, mirrored paragraph text and Hebrew fonts inside a layout that still flowed left-to-right. Re-measured on `4e1cf33` at 1440×900: the pill *did* move (top-right in Hebrew, top-left in English), and it was the only element on the screen that did.
- Cause: `direction` was set on `.brand-panel__inner`, `.form-panel` and `.lang-switch` — three boxes inside or beside the grid — and never on `html` or `body`. A grid container places its columns along **its own** inline axis, and `.auth-layout` inherited the root's `ltr`, so no descendant's `direction` could reorder its two columns. Setting direction on the children of a grid mirrors the text inside the cells and nothing about the cells. The first attempt did not diagnose this because it never looked past the one element in the bug report.
- Fix (`c6d53c5`): `direction` moved to the root — `html { direction: rtl }` with `html:has(#lang-en:checked) { direction: ltr }` — which is [§3.4](#34-bilingual--bidirectional-ui-hebrew-rtl-default--english-ltr) rule 1 rather than a fourth workaround. Six per-container declarations collapsed into two, and the four sibling text-swap rules plus the separate font-variable rule collapsed into the same root selector. Three physical properties that had been *invisible while nothing mirrored* went logical in the same pass: the `.reg-mark` bracket sides, `.form-panel`'s `border-left` accent, and its `border-left: none` in the ≤860px block. `lang.ts` now also syncs `<html lang>` / `<html dir>`. Verified in both languages on all three screens.
- Notes: **the lesson is that the first pass answered the bug report instead of the request.** The report named the language toggle, so the toggle got fixed — correctly, and it stayed fixed — while the sentence that actually specified the work ("the entire layout must switch direction") went unimplemented for a day. When a request names both a symptom and a requirement, the requirement is the acceptance test. Second, **two screenshots of the current build settle in a minute what a re-reading of the CSS argues about for much longer**: rendering `4e1cf33` in both languages showed immediately that the pill was fine and the grid was not, which redirected the whole second attempt. Third, the **corner-mark bug [§8](#8-known-issues--risks) recorded on 2026-08-20 could not be seen** until the page mirrored — an un-flipped layout hides its own direction defects, so fixing the mirror is what makes the remaining mirroring bugs findable. Fourth, the 2026-08-20 headless-screenshot trap below repeated exactly: Edge clamps `--window-size` and crops the PNG, so a 390px request rendered a **492px** viewport into a 390px image and read as mobile overflow; `documentElement.scrollWidth === clientWidth` disproved it in one measurement. **Chromium only** — `:has()` on the root and the logical border sides carry this fix and neither was checked in Safari or Firefox.

### [2026-08-25] A language toggle swapped its labels and never changed its side
- Symptom: on `login.html`, `forgot-password.html` and `reset-password.html` the language pill stayed in the **top-right corner in both languages**. Everything around it swapped correctly — strings, fonts, panel direction — so the screens read as properly bilingual and the one fixed element quietly did not participate. Measured at 1440×900 before the fix: Hebrew `right: 22px`, English `right: 22px`, identical to the pixel.
- Cause: `.lang-switch` was positioned with `inset-inline-end`, and **a logical inset on a fixed-position element resolves against that element's own direction**. The pill is a **sibling** of `.auth-layout`, while `direction: rtl` was set only on `.brand-panel__inner` and `.form-panel` — both *inside* it. So the pill inherited the root's `ltr` in both languages and `inset-inline-end` resolved to `right` every time. The identifying fact is that computed `direction` read back as **`ltr` while the page was in Hebrew**.
- Fix (`4e1cf33`): the pill now carries its own direction — `rtl` by default, `ltr` under `#lang-en:checked ~ .lang-switch` — so the logical inset finally has something to resolve against. `inset-inline-end` became `inset-inline-start` to put it on the **leading** corner, and `padding: 5px 8px 5px 10px` became `padding-block` / `padding-inline-start` / `padding-inline-end` so the wider side stays beside the globe once the contents mirror.
- Notes: **obeying §3.4 rule 2 is not the same as being in a direction** — this code used logical properties perfectly and was still wrong in one language, because rule 2 silently assumes the direction arrives from `<html>`, which is the debt rule 1 already records. The reusable check is not a grep for `left`/`right`: it is reading computed `direction` back in **both** languages and failing any element whose value does not change. **`register.html` has the identical defect and was not fixed** — see [§8](#8-known-issues--risks). Two adjacent traps this also uncovered: a four-value `padding` shorthand is a *physical* declaration that survives every logical-property audit, and `login.css` turned out to be the **auth stylesheet for three screens**, so "scoped to Login" needed asking about rather than assuming.

### [2026-08-23] One viewer stood in four incompatible relationships to the same project

**Problem.** Building My projects meant deciding, for every project the Personal dashboard names, what the
viewer's relationship to it actually is. `personal-dashboard.html` gave **four different answers for Herzl 8**:
*Pending actions* held an invitation to it awaiting the viewer's response, *Waiting on others* held an
invitation the viewer had **sent** into it (so the viewer owned it), *Overdue* held `Railing installation`
assigned to the viewer inside it, and a second *Waiting on others* row held the same task blocked by a
predecessor. The schema forbids the combination outright — the flow validates that
`assignee` is an accepted member — so a task cannot exist for someone who has not answered the invitation
yet, and a person cannot be invited to a project they own. Two of the four rows linked to `my-projects.html`,
so the screen could not stay neutral: it had to render Herzl 8 as **something**.

**Why it survived.** Each row is correct in isolation and the widgets are far apart in the file; the
contradiction is only visible when one screen has to state the relationship **once** for the whole project.
That is the same class of error the 2026-08-20 My Network / Browse pass caught by checking that no contractor
appears in a contradictory state on two screens — this is the project-level version of it, and no check
existed for it.

**Fix (`13c26dd`).** Herzl 8 **stays the invitation**, because that is the row the *Respond* control points at
and a Respond button whose destination has nothing to respond to is the dead control this project has already
rejected twice. The other three rows moved to projects the viewer genuinely has: the sent invitation to
*Private villa — Kfar Saba* and both `Railing installation` rows to *Logistics warehouse — Modi'in*, which
My projects renders as Owner cards, so a sent invitation and an assigned task are both legal there.
**No widget definition, no count and no product rule changed** — the fix is mock data, and the meanings of
*Pending actions*, *Waiting on others* and *Overdue* were deliberately left alone rather than widened until
the contradiction disappeared.

**Lesson.** A screen that aggregates *rows* can hold a contradiction indefinitely; a screen that states a
*relationship* cannot. Adding one is how you find the other. The check is now automated between the two
files — every dashboard row naming a project is compared against the group that project sits in on
My projects, and a row that acts inside an `Invited` project fails.

### [2026-08-23] The fix for a too-tall rail was an overlay, and the overlay covered the results
- Symptom: the advanced-filters panel opened *on top of* the contractor cards. Nothing was broken in the sense of being wrong on screen — the panel painted correctly, above the cards, exactly as its `z-index` asked — and that was the defect: the owner's requirement is that opening a filter must not hide the results it filters.
- Cause: the previous review had a real problem to solve — a rail taller than the viewport cannot stay pinned (see the two entries below) — and solved it by taking the refining fields **out of the layout entirely**: `position: absolute`, `inset-inline-start: calc(100% + 18px)`, hung off the rail. That does make the rail short, because an out-of-flow element contributes no height. It also means the panel occupies **no space**, so the results column never learns it is there and renders straight underneath it. The two properties are the same property: *out of flow* buys the height fix and costs the coverage.
- Fix: the panel and its scrim moved out of `<aside class="filters">` to become children of `.browse__body`, and `.browse__body:has(.adv-toggle:checked)` switches the grid from `268px 1fr` to `268px 268px minmax(0, 1fr)`. The panel is now a genuine grid column: it takes real space, the results reflow beside it to one card per row, and it stays short on its own terms — `position: sticky`, capped at `calc(100vh - var(--nav-h) - 44px)`, with `.adv-panel__body` scrolling internally. The rail keeps its own cap and its pinned Apply.
- Notes: **this also deletes the fix recorded on 2026-08-22 below** — `z-index: 5` on `.filters` was there to lift an overlay out of a sticky stacking context, and with no overlay left there is nothing to lift, so the line is gone. The entry stays because the lesson does. The reusable rule is narrower than "avoid `position: absolute`": **an out-of-flow element cannot make room for itself, so it is the wrong tool whenever the content beside it must move.** Reach for a grid track that appears on a state selector instead — `:has()` on the container makes that a one-rule change and needs no JavaScript. Second note, on `:target`: it was unavailable here because the profile panel already spends this screen's single `:target` ([§10 2026-08-20](#10-bug--issue-log)), which is why the opener is a checkbox and why the container needs `:has()` to see it. Third: `.card-grid` had `minmax(280px, 1fr)`, which **overflows a container narrower than 280px** rather than shrinking; it is now `minmax(min(280px, 100%), 1fr)`, because a results column that can be squeezed by a new sibling has to be able to reach any width.

### [2026-08-23] A visually-hidden label reported 285px of horizontal overflow
- Symptom: the responsive harness reported elements crossing the viewport edge on the Personal dashboard at every width from 480px down in English and 390px down in Hebrew, growing to eleven offenders at 320px. Nothing was visible on the screenshots, and `document.documentElement.scrollWidth` matched the viewport in every one of those configurations.
- Cause: the availability chip carried the full approved status wording as `<span class="sr-only"><span class="he-text">…</span><span class="en-text">…</span></span>`. `.sr-only` is a 1×1 box with `overflow: hidden` and `clip`, which hides the text **visually** — but the inner language `<span>` is a real element that lays itself out at its natural 285px and is merely painted-clipped by its parent. `getBoundingClientRect()` reports unclipped geometry, so the check saw a genuine 285px box hanging off the edge.
- Fix: the wrapper was removed and `.sr-only` moved **onto each language span** (`<span class="sr-only he-text">`), so the hidden text is a text node inside the 1×1 box with no child element to measure. The language toggle is unaffected — `.sr-only` sets no `display`, so the `he-text` / `en-text` rules still swap it. All 46 configurations then measured clean.
- Notes: **this was a false positive that was still worth fixing**, and both halves matter. It was false because nothing was visible and nothing scrolled; it was worth fixing because the noise would have masked a real overflow in the same report, and because the next person to audit this file would have had to re-derive the same conclusion. The reusable rule: **`.sr-only` hides paint, not layout — never nest an element inside it**, and any geometry check that walks `*` will find one if you do. Note also that the [§10 2026-08-20](#10-bug--issue-log) warning holds in the other direction here: `scrollWidth` said everything was fine, and only the element-rect check had anything to say at all.

### [2026-08-22] A sticky element silently trapped its own overlay behind the page
- Symptom: the new advanced-filters panel was given `z-index: 6` and sits inside the filter rail. It rendered *underneath* the contractor cards anyway — the first screenshot shows a card painted straight over it — even though the cards set no `z-index` at all and `6` should beat `auto` every time.
- Cause: **`position: sticky` creates a stacking context unconditionally**, with no `z-index` needed and nothing in the declaration to hint at it. `.filters` is sticky, so the panel's `z-index: 6` only ordered it *within the rail*; the rail as a whole then entered the page at `z-index: auto` and painted in DOM order, and the rail comes before the results. The panel could not escape its own ancestor however high its `z-index` went.
- Fix: one line — `z-index: 5` on `.filters` itself, so the rail outranks the results column. The panel's internal `z-index` was already correct and did not change.
- Notes: `position: relative` only creates a stacking context when a `z-index` is also set, which is where the intuition comes from; **`sticky` and `fixed` do it always**. The tell is that raising the child's `z-index` changes nothing — when that happens, stop tuning the child and look for a positioned ancestor. Worth remembering because the profile panel on this same screen is also sticky, so anything later given an overlay inside *it* will hit this identically. Found by rendering, not by reading: the CSS looks correct in isolation.

### [2026-08-22] A filter rail can be sticky and still scroll away
- Symptom: `.filters` had carried `position: sticky` since `97f3331` and the rail still scrolled off the top of the page. Nothing in the declaration was wrong, and it worked when the advanced group was collapsed.
- Cause: **an element taller than the viewport has nothing left to stick with.** Expanding the `<details>` group made the rail roughly 1,400px tall; once its height exceeds the viewport, the browser scrolls it normally until its bottom edge is reached, because there is no room to hold it at the offset. The property was never inert — the content had simply outgrown it.
- Fix: cap the rail at `calc(100vh - var(--nav-h) - 44px)` so it can never outgrow the screen, and move the refining fields out of it entirely. The card became a flex column with a fixed head and a fixed `.filters__foot`, so only the field list scrolls and Apply is always visible.
- Notes: the two halves of the original complaint — *"opening advanced filters makes me scroll"* and *"the rail scrolls away"* — were **one bug with one root cause**, which is why fixing the disclosure fixed the pinning for free. Verified by measurement rather than by eye: after `scrollTo(0, 600)` the rail pins at exactly `top: 88px` (`66px` navbar + `22px`) at 1440×900, 1440×816 and 1280×596, with Apply inside the viewport in all three. The corner registration ticks had to move from `.filters__card` to `.filters` in the same change, or they would have scrolled away with the content they are meant to frame.

### [2026-08-20] Two CSS-only controls on one screen both wanted `:target`
- Symptom: My Network was specified with tabbed groups **and** a confirmation dialog for removing a connection, both to be built with no JavaScript. Built the obvious way — `:target` for each — opening the dialog from the Connections tab would have dropped the page back to the default tab behind the overlay, and closing the dialog would not have returned to the tab the user was on.
- Cause: **a document has exactly one `:target` at a time.** It is the single URL fragment, so it is a *global* selection, not a per-component one. Two independent pieces of state cannot both live in it. This is not a bug in either pattern; it is the pattern's one hard limit, and it only appears when a second `:target` control is added to a screen that already has one.
- Fix: the two mechanisms were split by what each is actually for. **Tabs use four hidden radios** — a small one-of-N selection, which is the case the 2026-08-20 public-profile note already identified radios as suiting, and it is the same mechanism as the language toggle. **The dialog keeps `:target`**, which is the canonical CSS-only modal and needs no input element per row. The two no longer interact: opening the dialog leaves the checked radio alone, so closing it returns to the tab the user was on.
- Notes: the rule to carry forward is **at most one `:target` component per screen** — give it to the one that benefits from being addressable by URL or from not needing an input per instance, and drive everything else with radios or checkboxes. The public profile panel already spends `browse-contractors.html`'s `:target`, so a dialog added to that screen later hits this same wall.

### [2026-08-20] A headless screenshot reported a mobile overflow that did not exist
- Symptom: My Network was rendered at `--window-size=390,1000` to check the small breakpoint, and the screenshot showed the tab bar, the navbar and a row action running off the right edge — a textbook horizontal-overflow bug.
- Cause: **the browser never used a 390px viewport.** This Edge build clamps the window to a minimum of about 492 CSS pixels, so the page was laid out at 492px and the screenshot merely *cropped* that layout to 390px of pixels. Everything past 390px looked cut off because it had been cut off by the camera, not by the layout. Probing the page from inside confirmed it: `document.documentElement.clientWidth` reported **492** no matter what was requested, and `--force-device-scale-factor` did not move it either.
- Fix: the page is loaded into an `<iframe>` of an explicit width instead. An iframe establishes its own viewport, so media queries evaluate against the width set on the element and widths the window refuses to take — 390px, 375px, 360px, 320px — become testable. `--allow-file-access-from-files` is required for the parent to read into a `file://` iframe. The harness now walks 18 widths × both languages × all four tabs and reports any element crossing either edge.
- Notes: **two traps, and the second is worse than the first.** A cropped screenshot invents overflow that is not there; the same clamp would equally *hide* real overflow at a width the window will not take, and that failure is silent. Related: the check must measure **element geometry** (`getBoundingClientRect`), not `scrollWidth` — `body { overflow-x: hidden }` is set on every screen in this project, and a clipped overflow can report `scrollWidth === clientWidth`, so a scrollWidth-only check can pass a genuinely broken layout. The real 1px navbar overflow recorded below was found this way after the false one was dismissed.

### [2026-08-20] The shared navbar overflowed by 1px between 620px and 860px
- Symptom: at a 692px viewport the page was 693px wide — one pixel of horizontal overflow in English, with `.app-nav__actions` and `.lang-switch` as the elements crossing the edge. In Hebrew at the same width it fit.
- Cause: the navbar keeps its four links, the notifications bell, the profile chip and the language pill on **one row until 620px**, and they stop fitting well before that. The 860px breakpoint only shrinks the link padding and hides the profile name, which is not enough. It showed up in English first because English link labels are longer than their Hebrew equivalents — the exact asymmetry [§8](#8-known-issues--risks) already warns about, arriving as a 1px difference rather than an obvious break.
- Fix: the links move to their own row from **800px** instead of 620px, as a separate media query in `my-network.css`; the 620px block keeps only the page-padding change. Verified across 18 widths from 1440px down to 320px, in both languages and on all four tabs: no element crosses either edge.
- Notes: 1px of overflow is not cosmetic — `body { overflow-x: hidden }` turns it into silently clipped content rather than a scrollbar, which is why it survived to be found by measurement rather than by eye. The same 620px value is still in `browse-contractors.css`; recorded in §8 rather than reached across branches to fix.

### [2026-08-19] The database-design page reported a stale "Updated" date
- Symptom: opening `docs/database-design.html` showed **`Updated 2026-08-02`** in the masthead, suggesting the file had not changed in over two weeks — while its content was in fact current through 2026-08-18, carrying the `nationwide` region, its `$in` index-query consequence and the rating-anonymity notes.
- Cause: the date was a **hardcoded literal** in the chip. Nothing computed it, so it recorded the day somebody last typed it rather than the day the document last changed, and it drifted further with every documentation commit. The neighbouring chips did not go stale because `renderOverview()` fills them from the data.
- Fix: a tracked **`pre-commit` hook** (`.githooks/pre-commit`) stamps today's date into `<b id="stat-updated">` whenever `docs/database-design.html` is part of a commit, then re-stages the file — so the date is committed content. An earlier fix the same day, filling the chip at boot from `document.lastModified`, was **reverted**; see below.
- Notes: **`document.lastModified` is the wrong source for "when did this change".** It reports when the file was last *written*, not when its content last changed, and both halves of that gap were measured on this repo: simply opening the file in the IDE put the mtime **four hours ahead** of the last content commit while `git status` showed the content byte-identical; and because `git clone` writes every file at checkout time, a fresh clone reported the mtime as **the moment of cloning**. Anyone cloning the repo would therefore have read "Updated" as the day they cloned it, forever — the original defect relocated, not removed. Only Git knows when content changed, so the date must be written at commit time. The hook skips with a warning if the file also has unstaged edits, so it can never sweep unrelated work into a commit. `core.hooksPath` points at the versioned `.githooks/` rather than `.git/hooks`, which costs one `git config core.hooksPath .githooks` per clone; if the hook never runs the date degrades to **stale**, never to a confident wrong "today".

### [2026-08-19] Profile sections touched each other with no gap
- Symptom: on Edit Profile, Completed Work and Ratings ran into one another with no visible separation between the two panels.
- Cause: `.panel` had **no** vertical margin. Inside `.profile-grid__col` the column's `gap` separated panels, so the omission was invisible — but Completed Work, Ratings and Flexibility are top-level siblings with no flex gap to fall back on.
- Fix: `.panel { margin-block-end: 22px; }` in `profile.css`, with `.profile-grid__col > .panel { margin-block-end: 0; }` so grid columns keep using their gap and do not double the spacing. Logical property, so it holds in both directions.
- Notes: the trap is that a container-provided `gap` masks a missing margin until an element is used outside that container. Any panel moved out of the grid inherits this; the rule now covers it centrally rather than per section.
